| CMH-VAL-05 | NonShareable validation checks local companion refs, existing files, assets registry warning, version meta, missing-asset banner, watchdog, http(s) or protocol-relative remote URL rejection, non-`file` scheme rejection, local `file://` refs, and absolute-path warning. A `?query`/`#fragment` cache-buster on a companion ref (e.g. `commentable-html.js?v=1.7.0`) is stripped before the suffix detection and the on-disk existence check, matching what the browser fetches. `validate()` accepts an optional `base_dir` to resolve companion refs against the file's final location, or `None` to check structure only when placement is deferred. When a NonShareable document is explicitly requested, `new_document.py` defaults to absolute `file://` companion refs, while `--assets-relative`, `--copy-assets`, and `--assets-href` keep their explicit modes. `--assets-relative` REFUSES (exit 2, naming `--assets-href` and `--copy-assets`) when `--out` and the skill sit on different Windows drives or mounts, because no relative path exists across them - a broken relative ref, or a bare traceback, would both be worse answers. A `file://` companion ref's HOST is read the way the URL parser reads it, not as a literal: the parser percent-decodes a file host and maps it through domain-to-ASCII before the file-host state empties the exact string `localhost`, so `file://local%68ost/x` resolves to the same LOCAL path as `file://localhost/x` instead of a bogus UNC/SMB path whose on-disk existence check then reported a companion sitting right there as missing (the cased `file://LOCALHOST/x` always did, and is pinned as a control). The ref goes through the same input cleanup the network-URL predicate uses, so a BACKSLASH host terminator (`file://localhost\x`, which the parser ends at the `\` exactly as at a `/`) is local too. A TRAILING DOT stays outside that special case, so `file://localhost./x` keeps a real authority and is egress - the same call the network-URL predicate's `_PCT_LOCALHOST`, and the terminator its shared `file_network_arm` builds around it, make, and the two are pinned to agree about the `localhost` spelling. Only the PERCENT-ENCODING and CASE half of canonicalization is modelled: an IDNA/UTS-46 spelling that only maps onto `localhost` (`file://%EF%BD%8Cocalhost/x`) is read as a real authority on both sides, the accepted over-detection the network-URL predicate already records, and that predicate's drive-letter exclusion and its `..`/empty-segment over-detection are deliberately outside the parity claim. A `file:` companion ref that names no local path is reported as a FINDING, never as an uncaught exception and never as a wrong SCHEME. Two stages can throw on such a ref and both are guarded: `urlsplit` VALIDATES a bracketed authority, so `file://[foo]/x`, `file://[127.0.0.1]/x` and the unclosed `file://[::1/x` raise `ValueError` on EVERY platform before any host test runs, and `nturl2path.url2pathname` raises `OSError('Bad URL')` on an authority or path shape it cannot map (`file://[::1]/x` on Windows) - either used to kill `validate()` with a raw traceback and hand every fail-closed caller (`retrofit.py`, `content_replace.py`, `chart_block.py`, `finalize.py`) a traceback where a finding belongs. Resolving such a ref to `None` is not the fix either: that falls through to the non-file-scheme branch and blames the scheme, the one part of the ref that is right, so the resolver returns a distinct UNRESOLVABLE outcome and the finding stays cause-neutral (the ref does not resolve to a local file path). The HOST shapes that name no path - an IPv6 literal, a host:port, and its `|` spelling, which `nturl2path` reads as the same drive delimiter as `:` - are settled by the resolver ITSELF rather than by the platform's `url2pathname`, which raises on Windows, hands the string back unchanged on POSIX, and mangles a host:port into the bogus drive path `T:8080\x`; for those the verdict is identical on both platforms. An authority carried a slash deeper (`file:////host:8080/x`, which parses to a PATH under an empty authority) is screened the same way, since the resolver maps it the same way. A Windows DRIVE-LETTER prefix is exempt, so `file://C:/x`, `file://C|/x` and the separatorless `file://c:evil.example/x` the network-URL predicate also calls local still resolve and the guard widens nothing, but only the PREFIX is exempt: `file://c:evil:80/x` names no path, and an ENCODED colon (`file://C%3A/x`) is not a drive letter to the parser at all. Beyond the screened authorities the guarantee is crash-safety rather than parity: a PATH shape only one platform's resolver rejects (`file:///C:/dir/a:b/x.css`, or `file::/x` and `file:|x` where the drive delimiter LEADS the path) is a finding there and a missing companion elsewhere. The residual catch is by OUTCOME rather than by exception type, because the resolver signals a rejection as `OSError` in one shape and `IndexError` in another and 3.14 replaces the implementation outright, so an enumerated tuple is a list that goes stale into a crash. The caller CLASSIFIES a companion ref (remote, non-`file` scheme, drive letter) on that same parser-cleaned value rather than on the raw attribute, because those tests are ANCHORED: reading the raw attribute let the parser's own leading C0-or-space padding hide a scheme from all of them, so ` https://cdn/x.css` and ` vscode://x.js` fell through to the RELATIVE branch and were reported as a missing companion instead of as the remote or wrong-scheme reference the browser actually fetches. | `tests/test_validate_nonshareable.py` - `NonShareableTests` companion, meta, banner, watchdog, missing file, parent/subfolder, remote URL, protocol-relative URL, non-file scheme, absolute path, `test_file_url_companion_refs_validate_clean`, `test_percent_encoded_file_host_resolves_like_localhost`, `test_percent_encoded_localhost_companion_refs_validate_clean`, `test_trailing_dot_file_host_keeps_a_real_authority`, `test_trailing_dot_localhost_companion_ref_is_rejected_as_egress`, `test_file_host_locality_agrees_with_the_egress_predicate`, `test_malformed_file_host_companion_ref_reports_a_finding`, `test_malformed_file_host_verdict_does_not_depend_on_the_platform_resolver`, `test_a_raising_path_resolver_becomes_a_finding_too`, `test_a_resolver_rejected_path_shape_is_a_finding_not_a_traceback`, `test_an_unparseable_non_file_ref_still_reports_the_scheme`, `test_malformed_file_url_resolves_to_the_unresolvable_sentinel`, `test_nonshareable_detection_accepts_cache_busted_refs`, `test_cache_busted_companion_refs_validate_clean` tests; `tests/test_new_document.py` - `NonShareableCliTests.test_explicit_nonshareable_refs_resolve_to_dist`, `NonShareableCliTests.test_assets_relative_restores_relative_dist_refs`, `NonShareableCliTests.test_assets_relative_across_drives_exits_2_with_guidance`, `NonShareableCliTests.test_copy_assets_copies_companions_and_uses_bare_refs`, `NonShareableCliTests.test_assets_href_prefixes_refs`, `NonShareableCliTests.test_nonshareable_stdout_uses_absolute_file_urls` |
| CMH-VAL-06 | Chart validation runs automatically when a canvas exists and can also run chart-only or layer-only from the CLI. | `tests/test_validate_charts.py` - `ChartValidatorTests`; `tests/test_validate_cli.py` - `ValidateCliTests.test_layer_only_suppresses_chart_errors`, `ValidateCliTests.test_charts_only_suppresses_layer_errors`, `ValidateMainTests.test_help_flag_prints_usage`, `ValidateMainTests.test_double_dash_separator_treats_following_token_as_path` |
| CMH-VAL-07 | The validator covers raw diff markup errors, Kusto run-link warnings, and mermaid `cm-skip` advisories. | `tests/test_validate_kql.py` - `ValidateDiffAndKqlTests.test_diff_block_raw_html_errors_double_quoted_class`, `ValidateDiffAndKqlTests.test_kusto_run_link_valid_is_clean`, `ValidateDiffAndKqlTests.test_kql_figure_without_run_link_errors`; `tests/test_validate_layer.py` - `ValidateLayerStructureTests.test_mermaid_pre_without_cmskip_warns`, `ValidateLayerStructureTests.test_mermaid_with_cmskip_ok` |
| CMH-VAL-08 | The validator enforces the self-contained guarantee on the finished document: an `<img>`/`<script>`/`<iframe>` that loads over the network is an error (Chart.js CDN is an exempt opt-in in shareable mode), a fetching external `<link>` is a warning in shareable mode, non-fetching remote links such as canonical and alternate metadata are ignored, and a local-path `<img>` warns to inline it. A `<script>` is read for a network load through `src`, SVG2 `href`, AND the legacy `xlink:href` (`SCRIPT_LOAD_ATTRS`), in every mode, because an SVG `<script>` uses no `src` at all and its empty body left the load invisible to every other check; the set is namespace-blind for the reason CMH-OFFLINE-04 records, and the Chart.js exemption is bound to `src` alone, since `check_charts` only validates a `src` loader's pinned version and SRI - exempting an `href` would wave through a remote script nothing else inspects. WHICH scripts are read that way through `src` is decided by the script TYPE (issue #1144), because HTML decides it that way: "prepare the script element" classifies a `<script>` whose type is neither a JavaScript MIME type nor `module` nor `importmap` nor `speculationrules` as a DATA BLOCK and RETURNS before the fetch step, and for the two remaining KEYWORD types the `src` step fires an `error` event and returns, since external import maps and external speculation rule sets are not supported (a ruleset arrives inline or through the `Speculation-Rules` response header, never through `src`). So `<script type="application/json" src="https://host/x.json">` requests nothing at all, and reporting it refused a document over a load that never happens - `--strict` failed and the `commentable-html-validated` stamp was withheld for a dead attribute, which is the false-positive direction a gate cannot afford. It is the same KIND of reasoning `check_charts` and the offline active-data rules already apply - decide by TYPE - though `check_charts` reaches it through `script_external_load` / `script_code_runs`, the spec-exact predicate this row deliberately does not use, so on a residual spelling the two checks can disagree about the same element (this rule calls it a loader; `check_charts` does not, and may report that a canvas has no renderer). The predicate is `script_src_fetches`, and the RESIDUAL the earlier reading left is now closed (issue #1171). It used to be the type-only, deliberately over-inclusive `_is_executable_js`, chosen because THAT was the predicate PINNED to the exporter's `_offlineIsRunnableScriptType`, so the gate and the offline strip kept calling the same scripts loaders. The price was every shape whose type ESSENCE names JavaScript while a browser requests nothing: a MIME PARAMETER (`text/javascript; charset=utf-8`), `nomodule` on a classic script, a whitespace-only `type=" "`, and a non-JavaScript `language` fallback. Each was reported here as a network load AND had its whole element - body and all - deleted by the offline strip (CMH-OFFLINE-04 carried that cost on its side): a document refused over a request no browser makes, and an author's content removed behind the same verdict, which is the false-positive direction a gate that withholds the validated stamp cannot afford. The fix keeps the PAIRING rather than trading it away, because both sides moved in ONE change: the exporter grew `_offlineScriptSrcIsFetched`, a JS mirror of `script_src_fetches`, and `test_the_python_and_js_script_src_fetches_predicates_agree` pins the two over a shared corpus of ATTRIBUTE SETS - every namespace, the whitespace class, and every one of the shapes - evaluated in a real JS engine, comparing what the two shipping CALLERS compute rather than the predicates under matching assumptions. The question this arm asks is FETCH, not EXECUTION, and the difference is MEASURED rather than reasoned - which is the correction that separates this rule from a first attempt that read it through `script_code_runs` (CMH-VAL-27). Chromium issues the `src` request from its speculative PRELOAD SCANNER, which reads the tag soup ahead of the parser, so it differs from execution in two ways that a spec reading alone gets wrong. First, the legacy `event`+`for` pair does NOT stop the request: `<script type="text/javascript" for="x" event="y" src=...>` is REQUESTED and then never run, so skipping it here would have blessed a document that really does reach the network - the one direction a self-contained gate must never get wrong - and would have left a live reference in a zero-network export. Second, it is namespace-BLIND in BOTH directions: an SVG `<script src>` is requested even though SVG ignores `src` for loading, while an SVG `<script nomodule src>` is NOT requested even though `nomodule` is an HTMLScriptElement rule that an SVG script ignores for EXECUTION (an SVG `<script nomodule>` with an inline body does run). That blindness is why `script_src_fetches` takes no `ns` at all - there is no namespace to consult, by construction rather than by omission - and it is what lets this flat, namespace-less tokenizer answer the question exactly. The browser fact is not left as a claim: the `CMH-VAL-08: a browser requests exactly the script shapes the gate calls a load` spec drives every shape through a real engine, records which ones it actually requests, and compares that set to the shipped predicate's own verdicts, so an engine that ever changed its mind reds a test rather than silently invalidating this row. The namespace-blindness is MEASURED across the rules rather than extrapolated from one: the corpus carries the `language` fallback, the essence match and the whitespace-only `type` in the SVG and MathML namespaces too, so no rule is asserted for HTML and merely assumed elsewhere. `_is_executable_js` stays where over-inclusion is genuinely the safe direction, and that is recorded per caller rather than left implied: the offline inline-body EGRESS scan on both sides (a body it skips is a network import nobody looked at), and `_csp_predecessor_fetches`, whose question is "could anything before this policy fetch or execute" - over-inclusion there over-reports rather than blesses, and its input is a flat tag/attribute pair with no namespace. That both sides trim HTML's ASCII whitespace class rather than each engine's default is part of the same pin: `trim()` also takes NBSP and U+FEFF while `str.strip()` also takes U+001C-U+001F, so before this change a `type="&#xFEFF;text/javascript"` was runnable to the exporter and inert to the gate - a document the gate blesses and the export then deletes, the very drift this rule exists to prevent. An active-data block's `src` is still not ignored: offline mode rejects the BLOCK through the active-data rule below (a ruleset outright, a map because it carries a `src` at all), and that single error is the one an author acts on, where a second one about a request no browser makes would only send them after a load that does not exist. Only `src` is decided by type: the SVG `href` / `xlink:href` arm stays unconditional, since this tokenizer has no namespace to consult and an SVG `<script>` has no type-keyed data-block concept to read instead. The same false positive reached the CSP-PREDECESSOR rule, which decides whether a policy `<meta>` comes too late to be the document's guarantee: it counted a bare `<script src>` before the policy as a fetching predecessor, so a head-placed inert data block marked the policy late, the offline CSP requirement then saw no policy at all, and the document was refused with a different message for the very same non-request - `_csp_predecessor_fetches` now reads a script's `src` only through what EXECUTES, keeping the namespace-blind SVG arm unconditional. The exporter moved with the gate rather than after it (CMH-OFFLINE-04): `_offlineStripScriptLoad` removes a FETCHING script outright as before, takes only the dead ATTRIBUTE off a data block instead of deleting the author's data with it, and leaves an ACTIVE-DATA block's `src` untouched for the active-data pass that judges the block by it, so the export keeps no network-looking reference and never turns a map the source browser IGNORED into a live one. CMH-VAL-25 inherits the verdict element for element, so the nested spelling of an inert data block is clean too. For descriptor mode `offline`, every network-loading script, fetching link, image, iframe, media source, form target, meta refresh (whatever its target), CSS `@import`, and CSS `url(...)` is an error with no Chart.js exemption, the restrictive offline CSP is required, and data URLs remain valid. SHAREABLE mode covers the same AUTOMATIC SUBRESOURCE set as offline, and the SCOPE of that guarantee is a recorded decision rather than an accident of where a check happened to sit (issue #1145). It used to be enforced on five element/attribute groups only - `img` (`src`/`srcset`), `script` (`SCRIPT_LOAD_ATTRS`), `iframe` (`src`), a loading `link` (`href`) and `base` (`href`) - because the whole media/background/CSS set lived inside the `offline_mode` branch, so a shareable document carrying `<video src="https://evil.example/v.mp4">`, `<object data=...>`, `<embed src=...>`, `<input type="image" src=...>`, an SVG `<image>`/`<use>`/`<feImage>` reference, a legacy `background=` attribute, or CSS egress (an `@import` or a `url(...)` in a `<style>` block or a `style=` attribute) validated STRICT-CLEAN and received the `commentable-html-validated` stamp. That is precisely the promise the stamp makes to a RECIPIENT who did not author the file - that opening it does not reach the network - and, unlike offline, a shareable file has NO zero-network CSP behind it, so this gate is the only layer. The rule the scope is drawn on: the guarantee covers every network reach a browser makes when a reader merely OPENS the file, with no user action. What is deliberately OUT of scope, and stays offline-only, is USER-INITIATED egress - `<a href>` (already exempt for exactly this reason), `form action` / `formaction`, and `a`/`area` `ping`. Each of those needs a CLICK, so none of them happens on open; the offline rules that reject them serve the ZERO-NETWORK promise and are pinned to the export strips that remove them, which is a different contract from the self-contained one, and `ping` is rejected offline whatever its target for that strip-parity reason rather than because it is egress a self-contained file cannot have. A `meta http-equiv=refresh` is NOT in that group, and saying so is a correction the multi-duck review panel made to the first cut of this rule: `content="0;url=https://evil.example/"` fires the instant the document is parsed, with no click, so it is exactly "opening it reaches the network" and it is now an ERROR in shareable mode whenever its TARGET is a network URL (decided by the same shared `meta_refresh_navigates_to_network` reading the offline rule uses). Only a network target, unlike offline: a relative refresh reaches no network, and shareable mode has no export strip to keep unconditional parity with, so offline keeps its own stricter "every refresh, whatever its target" rule and the two never double-report one element. The offline-only HARDENING surfaces stay offline-only on the same reasoning: the required CSP, `on*` handlers, referrer policy, `srcdoc` presence, the active-data IMPORT MAP rule, and the inline-script import/navigation scans. The other half of that active-data rule did NOT stay: a `<script type="speculationrules">` moved into shareable mode, because a ruleset makes the browser prefetch or prerender with no user action and no code running, while an import map fetches nothing by itself and only matters through a module load. The speculative link rels needed NO widening here, and the reason is stated so a later reader does not re-open the question: `preconnect` and `dns-prefetch` are in `FETCHING_LINK_RELS`, so a NETWORK href on one is already the ordinary shareable `link` WARNING, and the offline-only extra is the PRESENCE rule (#1076), which rejects a hint whatever its href - a strip-parity rule, and moot for this guarantee because a relative hint reaches no network at all. The legacy `background` attribute is asked as an ATTRIBUTE question (`_find_attr_egress`) rather than over a hand-maintained tag list, because the export strip's own selector is the universal `[background]` and the list was NARROWER than it: it named `body`/`table`/`td`/`th`/`div` and so missed the table PARTS (`tr`, `tbody`, `thead`, `tfoot`), where the attribute really is a presentation hint that fetches, while carrying `div`, where a browser ignores it. Asking the attribute question makes the two sides agree by construction, removes the list as a maintenance surface, and keeps the over-detection identical on both sides, so neither can reject what the other leaves. Two further OVER-detections are deliberate and recorded rather than fixed, because narrowing either needs the parent chain a flat tag index does not have: a `<source src>` parked under a `<picture>` (where `src` is ignored) and a `<track src>` (which loads when a text track is enabled rather than on open) are both reported. A third joins them: a `<meta http-equiv=refresh>` parked in a `<template>` never fires, because a refresh is a PARSE-TIME pragma and adopting the fragment later does not navigate - unlike a template-parked `<img>`, which really does fetch the moment a script inserts it, so the template-inclusion argument that carries the element rules deliberately does not carry this one. Removing a reference that reaches no network costs an author nothing; missing one costs the recipient the guarantee. A `<script type="speculationrules">` is in scope for the same reason the refresh is: the browser reads the ruleset and prefetches or prerenders on its own, with no user action and no code running. It is rejected whatever the ruleset SAYS, exactly as offline rejects it - a `"source": "document"` ruleset names no URL at all and turns the document's own `<a href>` links, which this guarantee deliberately exempts because a reader has to CLICK them, into automatic fetches - so there is no body that can be read as safe. An IMPORT MAP is deliberately NOT reported in shareable mode: it re-points where a bare module specifier resolves and fetches nothing by itself, so it only matters through a module load, and offline keeps its stricter rule on it for export-strip parity alone. Widening the shareable side introduces no gate/strip drift, the failure mode CMH-OFFLINE-04 exists to prevent, because shareable mode runs NO exporter strip pass: the strips are an offline-export step, so this gate is the only implementation of the shareable rule and has nothing to stay in step with. What an offline report SAYS is unchanged for every check that MOVED - they pick their wording off `offline_mode` exactly as they did inside the branch - and `test_the_moved_checks_report_offline_exactly_once_with_unchanged_wording` pins both the exact wording and the absence of a duplicate. Two things about an offline report DO change, and neither is a wording change: the moved findings now precede the offline-only ones instead of being interleaved with them (nothing consumes the order), and the universal `background` read ADDS offline findings for the table parts the old tag list missed, which is the point of making it universal and is asserted by that same test. The widening is a BREAKING change for authored documents, stated as such in the changelog: a file that passed before may now fail `--strict`, be refused the stamp, and be refused outright by the fail-closed tools that run the validator first (`retrofit` / `upgrade` leave the target unchanged on any validator error). A relative or `data:` reference is untouched in every one of the newly covered shapes, so a document with no egress cannot start being rejected, and no shipped example or fixture gains a finding. Template-parked and `<noscript>` content is read exactly as the five original groups already read it (`<template><img src="https://...">` was an error in shareable mode before this change), so the widening introduces no new view, only more attributes read through the existing one. Every report line that interpolates an authored TAG NAME or attribute VALUE goes through `_report_value`, which bounds it and replaces the C0/C1 controls `str.split()` does not touch: a tag name may legally carry an ESC byte (HTML's tag-name class excludes only ASCII whitespace, `/`, `>` and NUL), and the universal `background` read is the first rule here whose tag name comes from the DOCUMENT rather than from a literal in this file, so an unsanitized line could have carried a live ANSI escape to a terminal. An `<image src>` / `<image srcset>` is read as the LOAD it really is, which took a paired move rather than a one-line widening (issue #1165). HTML's tree construction RENAMES an `<image>` start tag to `img` and reprocesses it with its attributes, so what a browser ends up with is an `HTMLImageElement` that fetches on open - while this gate reads the LITERAL tokenized name and `_MEDIA_LOAD_ATTRS` carried `image` only for the SVG `href`/`xlink:href` pair. The result was a complete INVERSION, measured on the #1145 branch: `<image src="https://evil.example/x.png">` drew 0 errors and was STAMPED while the byte-equivalent `<img src=...>` was refused, and `<image href="https://...">`, which in HTML content loads nothing at all, WAS reported. It affected offline mode as well as shareable, so it predated the widening. Widening the GATE alone would have been the CMH-OFFLINE-04 drift: in the DOM an HTML `<image>` IS an `img`, so the exporter's `all("img")` strip already cleared it, but an SVG-namespaced `<image src="https://...">` is matched by `all("image")`, which cleared only `href`/`xlink:href` - so a gate widened by itself would have REJECTED a file the exporter had just produced. The two therefore moved TOGETHER, the way #992 did it for `feImage` and #961 for the CSS patterns: `all("image")` now clears `src` and `srcset` beside both `href` spellings. The over-detection that rides along is identical on both sides - an SVG-namespaced `<image src>` fetches nothing and is reported and stripped anyway - and the reason it is KEPT is uniformity, not blindness: the namespace is reachable on both sides (the document walk computes it, and the DOM has `namespaceURI`), but the shared egress index deliberately does not carry it, and asking the same question on both sides is what makes the two agree by construction, exactly as the `<source src>`-under-`<picture>`, `<track src>` and universal-`background` over-detections this row already records do. Narrowing it would not be the drift this row warns about - drift is the GATE being stricter than the strip, so a narrower gate beside an over-clearing strip is the safe direction - it would simply buy a rarely-authored inert attribute at the cost of a second, namespace-aware notion of what an element is. What the move deliberately does NOT extend is the LOCAL-PATH advisory: the "run tools/inline_images.py" warning stays keyed on `img` `src`, and always has been - `img` `srcset`, an `<input type="image" src>` and every other local reference draw nothing either - so `<image src="photo.png">` is not a carve-out from a general local-path rule, it is that rule's existing scope, recorded here so a later "consistency fix" does not read it as an omission. TWO RESIDUALS are recorded rather than implied, both of them shapes that still reach the network from a stamped shareable file. (a) The CSS reading is the shared literal-pattern one. The `image-set(...)` half of that residual is now CLOSED for shareable mode (#1166): one, so a bare remote string needs no `url()` wrapper, so `image-set("https://evil.example/x.png" 1x)` in a `<style>` block, in a `style=` attribute or inside an `<iframe srcdoc>` was MEASURED validating STRICT-CLEAN and taking the stamp, and the candidate-list reader the deck gate already carried (#1155) moved into the shared `image-set(...)`, a CSS reading so the two gates ask one question rather than two that agree today. It is asked in SHAREABLE mode ONLY, and that scope is the decision rather than an omission: offline has a zero-network CSP behind the gate and shareable has none, which is exactly what makes the spelling reachable in shareable mode; widening the offline gate alone would make it reject a file the exporter's `_offlineCssNoNetwork` had just produced - the #961 precedent and the CMH-OFFLINE-04 drift - so the offline gate and the export strip stay in step by NOT moving, and the exporter and its cross-engine parity corpus are untouched by this change. The `url(...)` and `image-set(...)` readings run as INDEPENDENT tests of the same body, so a network `url()` in one rule cannot hide a network `image-set()` in another. Each candidate is read on ITS OWN and ANCHORED at its own start, the way `CSS_NETWORK_URL_RE` anchors immediately after `url(` and its optional quote, and a candidate that IS a function token is skipped as the other reading's to report - which is what makes one DECLARATION spelling both (`image-set(url(https://h/x) 1x)`) a single finding without any blanking pass. Reading the argument list as ONE string instead was wrong in both directions and both were MEASURED: a blanking pass could be made to swallow a later remote candidate (`image-set("x.png?q=url(" 1x, "https://evil/x.png" 2x)` validated clean, because the text `url(` inside a perfectly legal relative candidate is not a `url()` function), and an unanchored search reported a bare `data:image/svg+xml,<svg xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'>` candidate - which fetches nothing at all - as egress. An UNQUOTED `url(` whose contents carry whitespace is left alone deliberately: that is a bad-url-token, so a browser drops the whole declaration and no candidate in it is ever selected. What REMAINS residual is the CSS ESCAPE (`u\72l(...)`, `@im\70ort "https://..."`), escape (`u\72l(...)`), and a URL hidden by a comment used as CSS whitespace, and a candidate reached through a CUSTOM PROPERTY (`--u: "https://evil"; background-image: image-set(var(--u) 1x)`, which really fetches because `var()` substitution happens on the token stream BEFORE the property grammar is read - unlike `url(var(--x))`, which no browser supports since `url(` is a url-token rather than a function). The first two are literal-pattern gaps on BOTH sides, so covering them needs a CSS-TOKEN-aware reader on the gate AND on the exporter's strip in one change (#1029). The `var()` one is a deliberate call rather than an oversight: reading it needs custom-property resolution, and failing CLOSED on a bare `var(` would reject `image-set(var(--logo) 1x)` whose variable holds a local path - deleting an author's value over a reference that loads nothing, the one failure mode a gate that withholds the stamp cannot afford. All three are pinned by `test_the_recorded_shareable_css_residuals_are_knowingly_not_read` so a later change all invisible to the decision is deliberate. One BOUNDARY of the new reading is declared for the same reason: the OUTER scan for `image-set(` is a raw-text search like the `url()` reading beside it, so an `image-set()` written inside a CSS comment is reported though no browser fetches it (fail-CLOSED, and identical to what `url()` already did). Leading C0 controls the URL parser strips are NOT normalized, exactly as the shared `url()` pattern does not normalize them - that normalization is deck-local by design, so the two readings agree rather than one being quietly stronger. Until the token-aware reader lands a stamped shareable file can still fetch through the escape and comment spellings. They it. Those were closed as NOT PLANNED for OFFLINE (#1007, #1029) under the CMH-SEC-06 threat model, on the ground that the zero-network CSP - not the parser-level pattern - is what enforces fetch egress there; that reasoning does NOT carry to shareable mode, which has no CSP, so the escape, comment and `var()` spellings are recorded here this is a genuine shareable-only residual (tracked as KNOWN shareable-only gaps rather than quietly read as covered. #1166) and closing it means moving the gate and the exporter's `_offlineCssNoNetwork` together, the way #961 did. The same is true of a `<link rel="preload" as="image" imagesrcset>`, which fetches with no `href` at all and which #999 closed on the same offline-scoped reasoning. (b) Egress a SCRIPT performs at RUNTIME - a `fetch`, a dynamic `import("https://...")`, an assignment to `location` - is NOT enforced in shareable mode. That channel is UNDECIDABLE, and the offline scans that approximate it (`offline_script_navigates_to_network`, the inline-import pattern) are best-effort patterns kept there for export-strip parity; running them on the default path would reject a document for a string literal that merely LOOKS like a navigation, and a false rejection is the one failure mode a gate that withholds the stamp cannot afford. It is the same residual CMH-SEC-06 and CMH-OFFLINE-05 already declare for offline, restated here so the shareable promise is not read as stronger than it is: the guarantee covers what the DECLARATIVE markup does on open, not what authored script chooses to do. CMH-VAL-25 inherits the widening automatically, because the nested read shares `_MEDIA_LOAD_ATTRS` with the top-level set rather than restating it. A `srcset` is a LIST, so the gate reads it through HTML's own candidate state machine (`srcset_candidate_urls`, which mirrors the exporter's `_offlineSrcsetCandidateUrls` algorithm rather than its text, and is pinned to it over a shared corpus that compares the candidate LISTS and not only the verdicts) rather than by splitting on the comma: a `data:` URL carries a comma of its own, and the comma-split arm of the earlier two-reading union cut `srcset="data:text/plain,https://host/payload 1x"` into two candidates and rejected the second half - an error on a document with no egress at all, and one the exporter had already stripped for the same wrong reason (#1084). CMH-OFFLINE-04 records the tokenization, including the one step of HTML's algorithm both sides deliberately skip (descriptor VALIDATION, an over-detection kept because it fails closed); the two sides moved together so the gate cannot reject a file the exporter just produced. That offline scope is decided by the DECLARED descriptor mode ALONE, never by the NonShareable classification (#988). It used to read `not nonshareable and mode == "offline"`, which keyed the GATE to a lineage the exporter's offline STRIPS never consult: `saveOffline` reaches `_buildOfflineHtml` from the NonShareable path too (`_buildStandaloneHtml` inlines the companion CSS/JS and removes the NONSHAREABLE BOOTSTRAP block first, and the descriptor is then stamped `offline`), so a document that DECLARES offline while still carrying a companion reference - which a mangled or missing CSS/JS region marker leaves behind, and a hand-authored file can carry outright - switched OFF every rule in this paragraph at once: the zero-network CSP requirement, the media/form/background egress checks, the inline-script navigation and import scans, the active-data (importmap/speculationrules) rules, and the event-handler and meta-refresh gates. That is fail-OPEN in SCOPE, though not in verdict: such a document was never certified clean, because `_check_layer_descriptor` rejects the NonShareable-plus-offline pair on its own and did so before this change. What was lost was the REPORT and the second layer - a live `on*` handler and a missing offline CSP were not named at all, and the egress that WAS named carried the shareable wording, so the list an author worked from was incomplete and the rest of it appeared only once the descriptor line had been corrected - and the scope of a fail-closed gate should not depend on a different check happening to be right. That principle is applied where the widening is free, not absolutely: a descriptor that is MISSING, unparseable, not an object, or carries a mode outside the allowed set still leaves the offline rules off and is caught by the descriptor rule alone, deliberately, because defaulting those to offline would add wrong `offline mode:` errors to an ordinary shareable document whose descriptor merely failed to parse - a worse report, for no verdict, since none of those documents validates clean either way. The two sides are made to agree the way CMH-OFFLINE-05 settled the meta-refresh direction - the GATE widens to match the strips, rather than the exporter refusing an offline export on the NonShareable path, which would remove the only way a companion-file document becomes a single offline file. The check no longer even RECEIVES the classification (`_check_self_contained(html, parser)`), so it cannot regress to consulting it. The widening rejects nothing legitimately: the descriptor error and the offline errors are now reported together, and a document that declares `nonshareable` keeps its own rules unchanged - its companion `<link>`/`<script src>` stay ordinary local references, and the shipped bootstrap's `onclick` dismiss button (`dist/NONSHAREABLE.html`, `tests/fixtures/nonshareable/kitchen-sink.html`) stays clean, since no offline export ever keeps it. `<a href>` navigation is exempt. The META REFRESH gate decides its target by APPLYING the HTML shared declarative refresh steps (`meta_refresh_target`), not by matching `url=` in the raw attribute text, because a text scan was wrong in BOTH directions. The keyword and its `=` are OPTIONAL - after the time and its `;`, `,` or whitespace separator, anything that is not `url` is taken as the URL itself, so `content="0;https://evil.example"` navigates while a `url=`-anchored pattern saw nothing at all, a bypass CHEAPER than the scheme-only spelling this row was widened for; each letter of `url` is consumed before the next is tested, exactly as the algorithm advances, so a near-miss (`0;urhttps://host`) resumes where the match gave up. In the other direction a value with NO time is not a refresh at all (`url=https://evil`), a QUOTED value is TRUNCATED at its closing quote (`url='./x;url=https://evil'` is an ordinary local path), only ASCII whitespace is skipped (`url<NBSP>=...` makes the whole tail a relative reference), and a later `url=` inside a relative target's query string is just text - all four used to be reported as network egress. The extracted target is then handed to the SHARED `is_network_url` - the same predicate, after the same `normalize_url_value` cleanup, that every other egress gate in this row reads - rather than to a pattern of the refresh rule's own. That cleanup is the URL parser's (ASCII tab and newline removed anywhere, leading AND trailing C0-or-space removed, every backslash mapped onto a slash), so the prefixes a browser resolves to a network host all count: scheme plus slashes, protocol-relative, SCHEME-ONLY (a `https:`/`http:` with NO slashes after the colon, which the special-authority states resolve to the same host), a BACKSLASH authority (`\\host`, `/\host`, which a special scheme's relative-slash state treats like `//host`, resolving to a `file://host` UNC fetch off the machine), and the EXPLICIT `file://host` spelling of that same fetch. SHARING the predicate is the rule here rather than an implementation detail, because the bespoke copy drifted in BOTH directions the moment the shared one moved on without it (issue #979): it read exactly two leading separators, so the four-or-more-separator `file:////host` the attribute gate counts (a REAL AUTHORITY to Chromium, and the separator arithmetic is empirical because Chromium DEVIATES from the spec here: measured in Chromium 149 that URL parses to host `host` and re-serializes as `file://host/...`, where a spec-conformant parser takes the WHATWG file-host state's EMPTY host and leaves `//host/...` as the path - which is why the count is NOT `two or more`; the two counted arms are the BASE-INDEPENDENT set, since a zero- or one-separator spelling reaches a host only when parsed ABSOLUTE and inherits the base's host (so is local) against the `file:` base a document actually has - issue #1229 SETTLED that, see the paragraph below) was reported as local, and so was every slash run of three or more, which the shared `/{2,}` arm counts deliberately: what `///host` resolves to depends on the BASE - from a document SERVED over http/https (the marketplace site publishes these reports that way) the special-authority states ignore the run and it is that host, while from a `file:` base the file-host state takes an EMPTY host and it is a local path - so counting it is the fail-CLOSED reading, which here costs only wording. In the other direction a Windows DRIVE LETTER - which the file-host state turns into a path, so it reaches no host at all - was named a beacon that does not exist. The exclusions are therefore the shared ones: `localhost`, a drive letter (`file://C:/x`, `file://c|/x`), an empty authority (three separators after `file:`, and `url=https:` / `url=https://`, which are parse failures a browser does not navigate on, so reporting one would make the gate reject a file with no egress at all), and a single leading slash or backslash, which is a path. Issue #1229 asked whether any surface makes that inherited host reachable, and the answer is recorded here rather than left tracked: it does not, and the reason the LEADING run of a zero- or one-separator `file:` value cannot open an authority is structural. `file:` IS a special scheme - its backslashes are mapped exactly as any other special scheme's, so `file:\\evil.example/x` really is counted - but it does not take the special-authority-(ignore-)slashes states that collapse the slash-less `https:host/x` onto a host in the arm right beside it. The scheme state routes `file:` to the FILE state, which resolves against the document's BASE and reads the leading run as PATH, and EVERY surface that consumes this predicate resolves against that base: an attribute, a refresh target, and an `iframe srcdoc`, which inherits its parent's. A CSS `url()` is deliberately NOT in that list - the CSS readers are assembled from `CSS_NETWORK_PREFIX`, which carries no `file:` arm at all, so they never reach this question (issue #1230 tracks that separate gap). The refresh case was CAPTURED rather than reasoned about - a real Chromium navigating out of a `file:///C:/dir/report.html` document went to `file:///C:/dir/evil.example/x`, the ordinary local sibling an author means by `file:notes.html`, never the SMB share - and from a UNC base the value takes that document's OWN host, which it cannot choose, exactly as the plain relative `evil.example/x` beside it does. Two neighbouring contexts were measured rather than assumed, so the answer does not rest on the one case. A `<base href>` onto a non-`file:` base DOES rebase them onto a host the value names, and that is closed one layer up and unconditionally - a `<base href>` is held to the stricter `offline_is_non_local_ref` in EVERY mode and the offline strip removes it (the gap issue #924 closed; the still-open wording left beside the offline refresh rule was stale and is corrected). A document whose AMBIENT base is not a `file:` one - a report SERVED over http/https - does resolve them to a host the value names, and that case rests on the ENGINE rather than on this arm: a `file:` load from an http(s) document is refused outright (`Not allowed to load local resource`), measured for the already-counted two-separator spelling equally, so counting the short runs would buy nothing there while costing the false positive of calling an authored `file:notes.html` a beacon - the class the `localhost` and drive-letter exclusions exist to prevent, and the one a gate that withholds the `commentable-html-validated` stamp cannot afford. Both are Chromium measurements, like the closed scheme set, and nothing is claimed for other engines. The four-or-more reading is itself a WINDOWS one and the arm is deliberately fail-CLOSED about it: the same engine build parses `file:////evil.example/x` to host `evil.example` on Windows but to an EMPTY-host local path on Linux (measured in the pinned CI container), and the gate counts it on both, because Windows is where the UNC fetch exists. The exemption is scoped to the LEADING run and no further: a zero- or one-separator value whose PATH canonicalizes onto the four-separator form is still counted by the `..` and empty-path-segment arms (`file:/..//x.js`, `file:a//b.png` are corpus rows), which remain the deliberate base-less over-detection recorded above. The measurement is re-run on every CI pass rather than left as a claim in this row, comparing a real engine to this predicate's own verdicts, so an engine that ever changed its mind reds a test instead of silently invalidating it. A non-empty HOST is required, and that host test is a one-character approximation of the URL parser's, so a MALFORMED authority (`https://:80`) is still reported, the fail-CLOSED direction and the right one for a gate whose miss is a beacon. The RESIDUAL is now the shared predicate's own rather than this rule's - what `NETWORK_URL_RE` misses (a percent- or entity-encoded separator, a scheme assembled only at runtime) this misses identically - and it is bounded by the rejection above: since the exporter removes every refresh unconditionally and the validator rejects every one, a miss here costs only the WORDING of a rejection that still happens, never the rejection itself. This is the widening CMH-OFFLINE-05 gave the scripted-navigation literal, for the same reason: a meta refresh is a TOP-LEVEL NAVIGATION, the one channel no meta-delivered policy can close (`navigate-to` was dropped from CSP Level 3 and `sandbox` is ignored in a meta-delivered policy), so while the pattern required `url=` and the slashes, the whole channel was open to a one-token spelling change and `--strict` certified such a file as offline-clean. None of it introduces gate/strip drift, and the two now agree in BOTH directions rather than the gate merely staying looser: offline mode rejects EVERY `meta[http-equiv=refresh]`, exactly as the export removes every one whatever its URL (CMH-OFFLINE-05 records why that direction was chosen over the exporter keeping a relative one), and this target parser decides only WHICH message the rejection carries. A `javascript:` or `data:` target is therefore still not reported as network egress - mainstream engines refuse both as refresh targets - but the meta itself is rejected like any other. The NEIGHBOURING CSS network-literal gates - `CSS_NETWORK_URL_RE` and `CSS_NETWORK_IMPORT_RE`, the `@import` scan - now read the same widened shape (#961), and so does `NETWORK_URL_RE`, the ATTRIBUTE predicate the `_link_loads` path and every media/form/background check feed, whose parser-faithful input cleanup and `file:` authority arm landed earlier with #923. All three CONSUME the run of slashes after a special scheme rather than requiring two, because the special-authority states ignore that run, so `url(https:host/x.png)`, `@import "https:host/t.css"` and `src="https:host/x.js"` are caught as the network loads a browser resolves them to; and all three require a non-empty HOST, so an authority terminated at once - `url(https://)`, `url(//)`, a bare `https:` - stays local rather than being reported as a beacon it cannot be (which also closed a small pre-existing drift, where the CSS gate reported `url(//)` and the strip left it alone). Those are FETCH channels the required offline CSP closes IN OFFLINE MODE (`default-src 'none'`, `img-src data:`, `font-src data:`, and a `style-src 'unsafe-inline'` carrying no host source), unlike a top-level navigation - and that qualifier is load-bearing rather than pedantic: SHAREABLE mode requires no CSP at all, so there the literal gate is the ONLY layer and any narrowness in it is reachable, not merely theoretical. Issue #1230 measured that: the CSS readers carried no `file:` authority arm while the ATTRIBUTE predicate beside them had one (#923), so a shareable document with `url(file://evil.example/beacon.png)` passed `--strict`, earned the `commentable-html-validated` stamp, and made an SMB request off a Windows reader's machine on open. Both sides now read ONE shared arm (`file_network_arm` in `tools/validate/checks/resources.py`, mirrored as `_offFileNetworkArm` in the exporter and pinned to it as TEXT for every `stop` its callers use), so the separator arithmetic, the `localhost` and Windows drive-letter exclusions, the non-empty-authority rule and the two canonicalization arms cannot answer differently in a stylesheet than in an attribute; `CSS_NETWORK_START` is what the `url(...)`, `@import`, `image-set()`, anchored-candidate and deck readers are all assembled from, so no reader can be widened - or left behind - alone. Which characters END a CSS value is part of that arm, and the memberships are load-bearing: ASCII tab does NOT end one, because it is among the three characters the URL parser removes from anywhere, and counting it fired the `localhost` exclusion on `url("file://localhost<TAB>evil.example/x")`, whose host a browser reads as `localhostevil.example`; a raw LF or CR DOES, for a CSS reason rather than a URL one (it makes a bad-string token whose declaration a browser drops); and an OPEN paren does not, because a quoted string may legally contain one. What a PATH may CONTAIN is a DIFFERENT set from what ends a value, and conflating them was a measured bypass: the two canonicalization scans read the path, so scanning to a terminator truncated the scan and hid the popping segment behind it - `url("file:///a(/..//evil.example/x.png")` canonicalizes onto the four-separator UNC form and Chromium 151 requested `file://evil.example/x.png` for it while every CSS reader called it local. The scans read `_PATH_CHAR` and are bounded by `_PATH_SCAN_MAX` instead, which is what keeps them LINEAR: unbounded they are quadratic, and since these readers run unanchored over authored content while the exporter runs its mirror to convergence in the reader's browser, a sheet of repeated `url(file:a` (17.2s at 156 KB, 69.3s at 312 KB) was a hung tab. The cap's residual is bounded and recorded - a canonicalization-only spelling whose path runs past it before the popping segment is not scanned - and costs nothing on the channel this exists for, since the two AUTHORITY arms are exact and uncapped. Each is mirrored in the exporter's own JavaScript (`_OFFLINE_NETWORK_URL_RE` and the two CSS strips in `assets/js/68-export-offline.js`), so the widening had to land on BOTH sides in one change - widening the Python copy alone would make the gate REJECT a file the exporter had just produced, the CMH-OFFLINE-04 drift failure mode, where gate and strip have to move together. The CSS pair is pinned to the exporter's strips by a parity test that evaluates the runtime's own `_offlineCssNoNetwork` in node over a shared corpus, the same way the attribute predicate is - and the corpus carries the `file:` spellings in both directions, including the LOCAL controls (`url(file:///C:/x.png)`, `url(file://localhost/x.png)`, `url(file://C:/x.png)`, `url(file://localhost)`) and the tab- and space-padded ones, because a widening that deletes an author's local reference is the failure mode this gate can least afford. That CSP reason is only as strong as the CSP check itself, so the check now ENFORCES the premise rather than leaving a reader to take it on trust (#968); two halves of it used to be missing. (a) EXCLUSIVITY was enforced only for the directives whose required token is `'none'`. For `script-src`/`style-src`/`img-src`/`font-src` the check merely asked whether the required token was PRESENT, so `script-src 'unsafe-inline' https://evil.example; img-src data: https://evil.example` passed - and combined with the slashes-required shape above, a document carrying `<script src="https:evil.example/payload.js">` was certified offline-clean while a real browser fetched and EXECUTED it. The rule is now stated PER DIRECTIVE rather than left implicit: a `'none'` directive must be EXACTLY `'none'`, and each of the four fetch directives must contain its required token AND carry nothing outside a per-directive ALLOWLIST of source expressions that cannot fetch (`OFFLINE_CSP_ALLOWED`: `'unsafe-inline'`, `'unsafe-eval'`, `'wasm-unsafe-eval'`, `'unsafe-hashes'`, `'report-sample'`, `data:`, `blob:`). An allowlist rather than an exact match on the string the exporter emits, so a legitimate hand-authored policy is not rejected for no reason - and four shapes that LOOK inert are deliberately kept OUT of it, each for a stated reason: `'self'` (a `file://` document has an opaque origin, so what it matches is unspecified and has historically meant the containing directory - a fetch off the file), a HASH source (CSP3 matches a hash against an EXTERNAL script carrying `integrity`, so a hash in `script-src` is a network source), `'strict-dynamic'` (grants whatever an already-trusted script loads) and a NONCE (only meaningful for markup the author controls, and it propagates trust through `'strict-dynamic'`). Directive names and source expressions are both ASCII case-insensitive, so both are folded with `_ascii_lower` rather than `str.lower()` - a Unicode fold could map a non-ASCII look-alike onto a real token, and here that direction is fail-OPEN. A REPEATED directive is read the way a browser reads it, FIRST copy wins and every later one ignored, where the dict this was built from kept the LAST: a permissive first copy could be masked by a strict repeat written after it, and the browser enforces the permissive one. (b) PLACEMENT: the policy was read off the shared tag index, which records EVERY start tag, so a meta parked in an inert `<template>` - or written after the head is over, where the HTML pragma directives are not processed at all - satisfied the requirement while enforcing nothing (a `<noscript>` body was already excluded, since a scripting-enabled browser never creates the element inside it). The check now reads `_DocParser.csp_metas`, which records only a meta a browser really applies. Enforcement across several policies is CONJUNCTIVE - a resource must be allowed by every one - so ANY applied policy that meets the contract clears the check, and a failure reports the FIRST policy's shortfalls, the one an author wrote to be the document's policy. No gate/strip drift is introduced: the exporter emits exactly the required tokens and inserts them as `head.firstChild` (CMH-OFFLINE-05), so the policy it produces passes unchanged. Three more ways a policy could be read as enforcing something it does not are closed with it, each found by asking what a BROWSER does rather than what the string looks like. (c) The required set is not the whole policy, and an EXTRA directive may only TIGHTEN. CSP's more specific fetch directives OVERRIDE the ones this contract pins whenever they are present - `script-src-elem` decides a `<script src>` load rather than `script-src`, `style-src-elem` decides a stylesheet - and `worker-src`/`child-src`/`media-src`/`manifest-src`/`prefetch-src` are safe here only while they are ABSENT and fall back to `default-src 'none'`, so `script-src-elem https://evil.example` beside a compliant `script-src` re-opened the hole verbatim (and `worker-src` opens it with no attribute spelling at all, through `new Worker(...)` from the inline script the policy deliberately allows). The NAME set is therefore CLOSED rather than a list of dangerous names to enumerate, which is the only shape that stays fail-closed against the next directive CSP adds: a directive outside `OFFLINE_CSP_REQUIRED` is an error unless its source list is exactly `'none'` or empty, neither of which can widen anything. The directives that carry no SOURCE LIST at all are named in `OFFLINE_CSP_NON_FETCH` instead (`upgrade-insecure-requests`, `block-all-mixed-content`, `require-trusted-types-for`, `trusted-types`, `sandbox`), because running a sink group, a policy name or a sandbox flag through a source-list test asks a question their grammar does not have, and rejecting them would turn away a hand-authored file that layers on Trusted Types hardening. `report-uri`/`report-to` are deliberately NOT in that set, and the reason is stated exactly rather than overclaimed: a meta-delivered policy IGNORES them, so they enforce nothing and a document promising zero network has no use for a reporting endpoint - rejecting them keeps the emitted policy and the accepted one the same shape, and that same inertness (every effect a second policy could add is either conjunctive or ignored in a `<meta>`) is what makes the multi-policy short-circuit sound. It is also why `'report-sample'` is safe in the allowlist above: with no endpoint, a sample has nowhere to go. A required directive with an EMPTY source list is accepted for the same "matches nothing" reason `'none'` is, since reporting it as missing its token would reject a browser-equivalent policy. (d) The policy is TOKENIZED the way CSP tokenizes it, on ASCII whitespace only. Python's `str.split()` is Unicode-aware, so a NON-ASCII space - a NBSP - separated a directive name from its value here while a browser read the whole run as ONE unrecognized directive name and enforced nothing at all: one character per directive neutralized the entire policy while the gate reported it complete. Tokenizing the browser's way leaves the mangled name unmatched, so the required directive is simply MISSING and the document is rejected. (e) A policy meta that arrives LATE is reported rather than read. A meta-delivered policy is NOT retroactive - it governs only what the parser reaches after it - so a `<script>` that runs, a `<style>` that can `@import`, a FETCHING `<link>` or a `<noscript>` fallback written ABOVE it loads with no policy in force, which is exactly the channel the slashless spellings ride. Lateness is decided by CAPABILITY, not by tag name: the six predecessors that can do neither whatever their attributes are inert (`html`, `head`, `meta`, `title`, `base`, `template`), and a `<link>` is judged by whether its `rel` FETCHES and a `<script>` by whether it carries a load attribute or a type a browser RUNS - because a `rel=canonical` link loads nothing and a `type=application/json` block neither runs nor loads, so rejecting a policy written after one would be a false rejection carrying a message that claims something the element cannot do. (`meta` is inert here only because the one meta that DOES reach the network, a `http-equiv=refresh`, has its own offline gate rejecting every one of them; that gate is the single point of failure for this entry.) Three head-boundary details fall out of the same "what does a browser do" question and are settled in DIFFERENT directions, which is why this view is its own flag rather than the favicon check's `_head_ended`: non-whitespace CHARACTER DATA in the head pops the head and opens the body, so a meta written after it is a body child and its pragma never runs (and only ASCII whitespace counts, since a browser ends the head on a NBSP - `str.strip()` would have kept it open for exactly the character that closes it); an end tag named `body`, `html` or `br` is "anything else" in both the "in head" and "after head" modes and ends it the same way, while every OTHER end tag in those modes is IGNORED and must not (a `</div>` before the policy leaves it a head child); and `</head>` does NOT end it either, because the "after head" mode re-pushes the head element for a `base`/`link`/`meta`/`script`/`style`/`title`/`template` start tag, so a meta written there really is a head child and dropping it would report a document that HAS a policy as having none. The three obsolete elements the "in head" mode also holds (`basefont`, `bgsound`, `noframes`) are added to this view's head set rather than to `_HEAD_TAGS`, which deliberately mirrors `tools/authoring/_favicon.py`. What the OFFLINE CSS gate and the export strip gates still do not read is the CSS TOKENIZER's output: a CSS ESCAPE (`url(https\3a evil/x)`), a comment between the at-keyword and its URL, an embedded tab inside a quoted value, or a network reference carried by a bare string rather than a `url()`. (The BARE-STRING `image-set(...)` spelling is read by the SHAREABLE gate only - see residual (a) - which is why this pair is scoped to the offline side, where the strip is the thing it must stay in step with.) The exporter's strips do not read any of those either, so the two agree and the miss is shared rather than a drift; issue #1029 tracks giving both sides a CSS-token-aware reader. The one place they are NOT a fixed point is a reference written inside a CSS COMMENT: the gate reports it (raw text) and the strip deliberately leaves it, because consuming a `/*` would delete a comment's opener, leave its `*/`, and turn commented-out CSS into LIVE CSS - a fetch created by the strip itself, verified in a real Chromium. That is the same #1029 residual seen from the other side, and it is a loud `--strict` rejection rather than a silent beacon. Several shapes that WERE drift are closed here, because the gate reported them while the strip left them behind: an `@import` whose URL is followed by a media query, a `layer()`/`supports()` clause or nothing at all (the strip now consumes the at-rule's prelude to its `;`, block boundary or end of sheet, as a CSS parser does, stopping at the next `@` so a LOCAL `@import` after a network one survives), a QUOTED value carrying a `)` or the other quote character (the strip now reads a quoted string as a string), an unterminated `url(` token or an `@import` string that is never closed (both are consumed the way a browser consumes a bad-url token; the `url(` shape was verified FETCHING in a real Chromium), and `@import"https://host/x.css"` with NO whitespace after the at-keyword (a `"` cannot continue an ident, so that is a valid at-rule a browser fetches). The strip also runs to CONVERGENCE and replaces a removed at-rule with a space, since a deletion can otherwise splice two inert fragments into a live `@import`. Both sides also spell their whitespace out as the ASCII set rather than `\s`, since a JS `\s` takes U+00A0 and U+FEFF where Python's does not and neither is CSS whitespace, and both skip the padding the URL parser strips from inside a quoted value. The pair set this rule enforces is now PINNED to the other two implementations of the same contract rather than kept in step by hand (issue #1179, CMH-BUILD-22): `tests/test_egress_list_parity.py` reads `_MEDIA_LOAD_ATTRS` plus the core groups above out of this module, the deck gate's `_EGRESS_ATTRS` / `_EGRESS_ANY_ATTRS` out of `deck/deck_validate.py`, and the offline exporter's strip passes out of `assets/js/68-export-offline.js`, and fails when a widening lands on one side only - every intentional difference (the deck's outright ban on `iframe`/`object`/`embed` and on any external `<script>`, its untyped `<input src>` read, and the exporter's `feImage` selector spelling) is named in that test as data with a reason rather than silently excluded. It is the mechanical version of what a human or a review panel caught one spelling at a time in #992, #961 and #1165, and the first two divergences it found were real. The deck gate had no `feimage` rule at all, so a deck could still fetch through the SVG filter primitive this gate has reported since #992. And the deck treated the legacy `lowsrc` as a fetch on ANY element while this gate and the exporter carried no rule for it: that one was settled by MEASUREMENT rather than by a second unmeasured claim - in Chromium, with the `lowsrc` image FIRST in the document, a plain `src` and a legacy `background` are both requested and the `lowsrc` is not requested at all - so the deck's rule was retired to match this gate, and reviving it means moving all three lists together. The same reading reaches an SVG PRESENTATION ATTRIBUTE, whose value is a CSS declaration value rather than a URL (issue #1186): `<rect clip-path="url(https://host/x.svg#c)">` is neither a (tag, attribute) pair the element rules read nor part of a stylesheet the `style=` / `<style>` reads inspect, so a document carrying one validated STRICT-CLEAN, was given the `commentable-html-validated` stamp, and fetched the moment a recipient opened it - and in shareable mode there is no CSP behind the gate, so this reading is the only layer. The attributes are asked as the universal ATTRIBUTE question (`_find_attr_egress`) for the same reason `background` is, and judged by the shared `CSS_NETWORK_URL_RE` in BOTH modes, with the shareable-only `image-set(...)` reading beside it. That second reading is deliberately NARROWER than the `url()` one - `SVG_IMAGE_SET_PRESENTATION_ATTRS`, today `mask` and `cursor` - because only an attribute that takes an IMAGE fetches a bare remote string with no `url()` wrapper: `fill`, `stroke`, `clip-path`, `filter` and the markers take a paint server, a shape reference or a filter and request nothing (measured), so reading a candidate on them would REFUSE a document with no egress at all. The narrower list is held to the measurement in BOTH directions - every attribute the `url()` list enforces is answered by the image-set measurement as a fetch or as inert - because the first cut of it said "only `mask`" and left `cursor`, an image-taking attribute added in the same round, blessed and fetching (round-2 review panel, 4 of 8 ducks). Its shareable-only scope is the same split this row already records for `style=`, and the claim under it - that offline the zero-network CSP, not the strip, is what stops the fetch - is now PROVEN live rather than asserted. WHICH attributes are in scope is decided by MEASUREMENT rather than by the list of properties the specs say accept a `<url>`: in Chromium 149, with every request routed and aborted, the negative probes first in the document and the page settled to network-idle, `clip-path`, `mask`, `fill`, `stroke`, `marker-start`, `marker-mid`, `marker-end` and `cursor` are REQUESTED while `filter`, `mask-image`, `mask-border-source`, the `marker` shorthand and `color-profile` are not. The probe SHAPE decides the answer, so each attribute is probed the way the property is really used rather than all of them in one shape: `cursor` needs a fallback keyword (`url(...), auto`) to be a valid declaration at all, and measured INERT while it was probed without one, when a valid one really is fetched (round-1 review panel). `filter` is carried anyway - Chromium REMOVED external filter references, so its negative is an engine decision rather than a structural one, and a rule that fires only on a NETWORK reference costs an author with a local `url(#f)` nothing - while the other measured negatives are carried by nobody, on the `lowsrc` precedent (CMH-DECK-04). A LOCAL `url(#clip)`, which is how these attributes are almost always written, is untouched. The reading is the SHARED pattern, so it inherits that pattern's declared literal residual unchanged (a CSS-escaped scheme or function name, a reference written inside a comment): a presentation attribute is neither better nor worse covered than a `style=` attribute, and closing that residual is one change across all three CSS surfaces at once, not a per-attribute one. Scope is the ATTRIBUTE, not the property: an SMIL animation element that supplies the same property's value indirectly (`<set attributeName="mask" to="url(https://host/x.svg#m)">`, and the `to` / `from` / `by` / `values` of `<animate>`) is measured to fetch and is NOT read here - a different surface with its own `attributeName` indirection, tracked as its own issue rather than folded in, so nothing in this row should be read as closing the whole property class in shareable mode. | `tests/test_validate_self_contained.py` - `NewCheckTests` external img/script/link, data-URI, | CMH-VAL-09 | The validator reports every offending instance in one pass (no early exit): each raw-HTML diff block, each unlabeled `<canvas>` counted together, each `embeddedComments` item with a missing/unsafe id, and duplicate heading ids. `--strict` exits non-zero when any warning remains, so a single run surfaces everything to fix. | `tests/test_validate_kql.py` - `test_diff_block_reports_every_bad_block_in_one_pass`; `tests/test_validate_self_contained.py` - `NewCheckTests` canvas-report-all, embedded-item-id, duplicate-heading, and `--strict` tests |
| CMH-VAL-10 | The validator errors when the REAL parsed `<body>` open tag carries a transient runtime UI-state class (`sidebar-open`, `cm-sidebar-resizing`, or `cm-widget-dragging`): those are toggled on `document.body` at runtime and must never persist in a shipped file (a baked `sidebar-open` renders the content full width with an empty sidebar gutter via the `body.sidebar-open .app` rule before the layer re-derives state on load). The check reads the parsed `<body>` element's attributes (not a raw first-token regex), so a `<body class="sidebar-open">` decoy inside a `<head>` script or comment before the real body is ignored, a real dirty body after such a decoy is still caught, and a legitimate CSS/JS reference to `sidebar-open` is not flagged. | `tests/test_validate_kind.py` - `test_sidebar_open_body_class_errors`, `test_sidebar_open_only_in_css_or_js_is_clean`, `test_sidebar_open_decoy_before_real_body_is_clean`, `test_sidebar_open_real_body_after_decoy_errors` |
| CMH-SYN-01 | `tools/validate/validate.py` fails a mermaid sequenceDiagram whose message contains a `;` that splits it into a broken second statement. Once the first `;`-segment is a real signal, the ONLY valid tails are another full signal, a keyword-led statement, or an empty (trailing `;`) tail; ANY other tail - an arrow-without-colon signal, a bare word, or ordinary prose (e.g. `A->>B: hi; take entities + dict bags as-is`) - is flagged, which mermaid rejects as "Syntax error in text". Calibrated to zero false positives: only a NON-keyword-led, non-comment, non-empty tail that is not itself a valid signal is flagged (so a `participant A as "a->b"` alias, a keyword tail, and a valid multi-signal `A->>B: x; C->>D: y` are never flagged); runs as a content check inside `validate()`. | `tests/test_validate_mermaid.py` - `MermaidSequenceRule` (`test_flags_original_bug`, `test_valid_multi_signal_not_flagged`, `test_arrow_in_message_without_semicolon_not_flagged`, `test_participant_alias_with_arrow_not_flagged`, `test_semicolon_then_keyword_not_flagged`, `test_bare_word_tail_is_flagged`, `test_prose_tail_repro_is_flagged`), `MermaidWiredIntoValidate.test_validate_flags_broken_sequence_in_document` |
| CMH-SYN-02 | The Python mermaid checker deep-checks ONLY `sequenceDiagram`; every other diagram family (flowchart, graph, class, state, er, gantt, pie, ...) is recognized but delegated to the repo-side real-parser oracle, so it is never a Python false positive - including constructs earlier heuristics mis-flagged: a flowchart label containing `%%` or a literal `"` in a slash/round/hex shape, or a `click` callback. Within `sequenceDiagram` it stays zero-false-positive by only inspecting a line whose first `;`-segment is a real signal (never a keyword-led directive such as `accTitle:`/`accDescr:`, matched even with no space after the colon and even when its free text contains a post-arrow colon), only flagging non-keyword-led tails (so a `participant`/`activate`/`Note`/`link`/`accTitle:` tail is fine), and neutralizing `%%{init}%%` directives and mermaid character entities (`#59;`, `#quot;`) before the split while treating a line or `;`-segment that starts with `%%`, a single `%`, or `#` as a comment that consumes the rest of the line (mid-line `%`/`#` is literal, and an entity like `#quot;` is decoded first so an entity-led tail is still inspected). Unknown types and already-rendered `<svg>` blocks are skipped, an empty block is flagged (it renders as mermaid's "No diagram type detected" error), and text parked in a `<template>` nested inside a live mermaid host is not diagram source at all (it lives in the template's inert DocumentFragment, outside the host's `textContent`), so it is never syntax-checked - see CMH-CONTENT-16. | `tests/test_validate_mermaid.py` - `MermaidFlowchartDelegatedToOracle.test_flowchart_never_flagged`, `MermaidSequenceRule` (`test_acctitle_directive_not_flagged`, `test_inline_init_directive_not_flagged`), `MermaidRound2Regressions` (`test_acctitle_post_arrow_colon_not_flagged`, `test_acctitle_no_space_not_flagged`, `test_numeric_entity_semicolon_not_flagged`, `test_named_entity_semicolon_not_flagged`, `test_midline_percent_before_colon_not_flagged`, `test_keyword_tail_link_not_flagged`, `test_still_flags_the_real_bug`, `test_comment_tail_after_semicolon_not_flagged`), `MermaidRound5Regressions` (`test_percent_comment_tail_not_flagged`, `test_hash_comment_tail_not_flagged`, `test_comment_leader_consumes_rest_of_line`, `test_linestart_single_comment_not_flagged`, `test_entity_led_tail_is_still_flagged`, `test_still_flags_the_real_bug`), `MermaidTypeGating` (`test_other_types_not_flagged`, `test_rendered_block_skipped`, `test_empty_block_is_flagged`), `MermaidTemplateInertness` (`test_template_parked_text_is_not_diagram_source`, `test_unclosed_template_stays_inert_to_eof`, `test_live_text_around_a_template_is_still_checked`, `test_a_block_whose_only_text_is_parked_is_empty`, `test_a_foreign_template_hides_nothing`) |
| CMH-SYN-03 | The shipped Python mermaid checker has ZERO false positives, proven against a broad (200+ entry) corpus spanning every mermaid diagram family (`tests/fixtures/mermaid-corpus.json`, bulk sources in `mermaid-corpus.src.json`): each entry's `valid` is stamped by the REAL mermaid parser and its `py_flag` by the REAL Python checker, and `build_mermaid_corpus.mjs` FAILS generation if the checker flags any parser-valid diagram, so the checker never flags a diagram the real parser accepts.
