| CMH-VAL-21 | ONE set of element boundaries decides what the validator sees, for every check and on every interpreter. Both tolerant passes over a document - `_DocParser` (the view the chart, link, id, heading, meta, anchor, `<style>`/`<script>` and content-region checks read) and `_CodeSpanParser` (the `<pre>`/`<code>` spans of CMH-VAL-11 / CMH-KQL-08) - derive from a shared `_BrowserBoundaries` base that applies the boundaries a BROWSER applies, EXPLICITLY, rather than inheriting whatever tables the host `html.parser` happens to ship. The document parser previously kept the blind spots the code-block tokenizer had already closed, so the same file was two different documents depending on which check asked and which Python ran it. Shared and applied identically by both: (1) the whole raw-text / RCDATA set (`script`, `style`, `textarea`, `title`, `xmp`, `iframe`, `noembed`, `noframes`, `noscript`) holds TEXT in the HTML namespace, so a `<canvas>`, an `<a>`, an `id` or a heading a reader only SEES quoted inside one contributes no element, and an unclosed one runs to end of document instead of swallowing markup selectively - while NOTHING is raw text inside FOREIGN content, because HTML5's "in foreign content" insertion mode takes even a `<script>` or `<style>` start tag through "any other start tag", inserting a foreign element and leaving the TOKENIZER in the data state, so `<svg><script><img src=...></script></svg>` really does build the `img` (a breakout tag pops the open foreign elements and inserts it in the HTML namespace) and fetch it, as Chromium confirms; reading such a body as text hid that live element from the tag lookup AND the document parse, the exact pair the self-contained and offline resource gates read, so a document that makes a network request was certified self-contained. Because that reading also makes a `<script>`/`<style>` there able to CONTAIN one and to be popped early by a breakout start tag, each `<script>`/`<style>` BODY capture is a STACK keyed by element depth, finalized from `_truncate_stacks()` (its own end tag, an ancestor's, a breakout, or end of input) rather than by end-tag NAME, with data appended to the capture whose element is the CURRENT NODE only - a browser reads an element's CSS or code from its own CHILD text nodes, and crediting a nested element's text to the ancestor would let text no browser treats as its source satisfy a PRESENCE check, which fails OPEN: one scalar per kind let an inner element replace the outer capture, so the outer body - CSS a browser still applies, `@import url(...)` included - reached `styles` in neither element and its egress was invisible to the offline gate, and a stray `</style>` could end a capture no element of it ever opened. The template-parked view is a depth-keyed stack finalized the same way, so a script NESTED in a parked one is recorded with its OWN attributes (the export path walks every `<script>` in a template, so folding an inner EXECUTABLE script into an outer inert record let the offline gate skip a network import the exporter carries), the recorded bodies are restored to DOCUMENT order (nested captures finalize innermost first), and the NonShareable watchdog token counts only from a script whose element is the current node AND that OPENED outside the content region, so one the author left open ACROSS the content-end marker cannot stand in for the layer's; (2) a raw-text element closes on `</name` followed by whitespace, `/` or `>` (`</script data-x>`, `</script/>`), and the END TAG then ends at the first `>` that is not inside a quoted attribute VALUE - a value exists only after `=`, so a bare quote where an attribute NAME belongs (`</script " >`) neither ends the tag early nor makes it look unfinished, which used to run the region on to the document's next canonical closer and hide the author's real code block from CMH-VAL-11; (3) a comment closes at `-->`, at the legacy `--!>`, and abruptly at `<!-->` / `<!--->` - and at nothing else, in particular NOT at `-- >`, which `_markupbase._commentclose` accepts before 3.13 (a false close resurrects commented-out markup as live elements); (4) an unterminated comment consumes the rest of the document, as a browser does, instead of resuming after the next `>`; (5) `<![CDATA[ ... ]]>` is a section only when the CURRENT NODE is foreign (`svg`/`math`) - elsewhere, including under an HTML integration point or after a breakout start tag, it is a bogus comment ending at the first `>` and the markup after it is LIVE; (6) `<plaintext>` is the raw-text element a browser NEVER leaves, so it swallows the rest of the document even past a `</plaintext>` that looks like a closer (html.parser has no plaintext mode before 3.13); (7) a tag name AND an attribute name fold ASCII-case-insensitively ONLY, so `</\u017fcript>` does not close a `<script>` and - U+212A KELVIN SIGN being the one character outside ASCII whose `str.lower()` IS an ASCII letter ("k") - `<lin\u212a>` is not a `<link>`, `</mar\u212a>` does not close a `<mark>` and `data-\u212aey` is not `data-key`. `html.parser` hands every handler the UNICODE fold, and the unfolded name survives only in the SOURCE, so it is re-folded here: attribute names inside the one shared `_browser_attrs()` helper every attribute view is built from, and tag names through a `_BrowserTagNames` base that stashes the raw name as each start or end tag is parsed and that `_BrowserBoundaries`, the `_TagAttrParser` lookup and the checklist / notes / density passes all derive from - so no check in this package can key on an element or attribute a browser does not have. (The scanners OUTSIDE the `checks` package - the contrast scanner, the deck validator, the checklist and notes appliers and the authoring tools - fold ASCII-only too, since they derive from the same base: through `_browser_boundaries.BrowserBoundaries` and `_browser_attrs.StartTagParser` where a scanner needs the element boundaries or the start-tag extent, and through `_browser_attrs.BrowserTagNames` - the same class, re-exported - where it needs only the name. The shim's `ascii_lower()` folds the names in the few attribute maps and CSS selectors a tool still builds itself, and the passes that REWRITE markup on a REGEX tag or attribute name rather than through a parser - the deck font fixer's remote-`<link>` strip, the Shareable conversion's companion, raw-text and descriptor scans, and the upgrade masker's raw-text closer - carry `re.ASCII` beside `re.IGNORECASE` and assert the HTML name terminator rather than `\b` on the OPENING tag, since Python's IGNORECASE folds U+212A onto "k" and U+017F onto "s", and an ASCII `\b` in turn calls any non-ASCII letter a boundary - so bare `re.I` deletes a `<lin\u212a>` and `\b` under `re.A` deletes a `<link\u212a>`, neither of which a browser loads anything for. A CLOSER keeps the tolerant `\s*[^>]*>` tail (`</script >` and `</script foo>` really do close the element): a span is only entered through a real opening tag, so a closer that matches slightly more than a browser ends that span early rather than rewriting an element a browser does not have. The guards that only REFUSE to write or escape MORE keep the wider fold, since over-matching costs nothing there while a pass that over-matches corrupts the document. A structural gate fails a NEW scanner there that derives straight from `html.parser.HTMLParser` without supplying the fold itself.) (No raw-text element name contains a "k", so the raw-text region the host itself opens cannot be spoofed this way.) ASCII case still folds, so `<DIV ID="x">` is the same element and attribute as `<div id="x">`; and (8) the host's RCDATA character-reference decoding (3.13 decodes inside `title`/`textarea`, 3.12 has no such notion) is dropped, so raw text reads the same everywhere; and (9) an ATTRIBUTE VALUE's character references are decoded by the BROWSER rule rather than by the host: a NUMERIC reference always resolves, and it resolves through the HTML "numeric character reference end state" implemented here (`_numeric_charref()`) rather than through `html.unescape()`, which is NOT the browser rule and disagrees with it identically on every interpreter (so this is a validator-vs-browser gap, not the 3.12/3.13 drift): `html.unescape` DELETES the code points it deems invalid, so `&#1;`, `&#x7f;`, `&#x8d;` and the noncharacters vanished where a browser keeps U+0001, U+007F, U+008D and U+FFFE - a validator-visible `id`, `href`, `content` or `data-*` that differs from the DOM value, which is the very class of mismatch this rule exists to close. The end state resolves the null character, a surrogate and anything past U+10FFFF to U+FFFD, applies the C1 (0x80-0x9F) replacement table, and KEEPS every other code point; its digit run is BOUNDED before any integer conversion, so an OVERSIZED reference (more digits than Python's integer conversion limit, on which `html.unescape` RAISES) resolves cheaply to U+FFFD, as a browser renders it, instead of being left literal. The host decodes attribute values inside its OWN `parse_starttag()` and raises there first, and every parse entry point swallowed that into a TRUNCATED parse - one oversized reference hid every finding after its tag - so the shared `_BrowserStartTag` base takes such a tag away from the host BEFORE it decodes anything and dispatches it from its RAW text through the vendored tokenizer, leaving the rest of the document live. Detecting the reference up front rather than CATCHING the host's `ValueError` is what makes that safe: the host calls `handle_starttag()` / `handle_startendtag()` / `handle_data()` INSIDE `parse_starttag()`, so a catch would also swallow a subclass handler's own `ValueError` and dispatch that tag a SECOND time; it also keeps the host from building a big integer for a huge HEX reference (which has no digit limit) whose value is then discarded. The per-tag probe is gated on one memoized scan of the buffer, so a document with no such reference - every real one - pays nothing, and the routing threshold sits far above anything an authored document contains and far below the interpreter's own limit, so ordinary markup (a merely zero-padded `&#0000065;`) is never routed onto a second tokenizer that would decide "is this tag an element, and where does it end" by a different rule than the rest of the document. The dispatch tail mirrors the host's EXACTLY: start-vs-startend is decided by the tail attribute tokenization STOPPED at, never by the raw text (an unquoted value swallows a trailing `/`, so `<div a=b/>` is a normal start tag whose value is `b/` - reading the raw text instead self-closed it and skipped the raw-text mode below, which exposed a `<script>` or `<textarea>` body to the checks as live markup), and the host's own CDATA / RCDATA / `plaintext` / `noscript` branch is re-entered, so a tag taken off the recovery path enters exactly the mode every other tag in the same parser enters. SCOPE: the attribute path is what `_BrowserStartTag` covers, because the host decodes attribute values inside `parse_starttag()`. The document's TEXT is decoded by the SAME bounded rule: the same `html.unescape()` also decodes every text run inside the host's `goahead()`, so one oversized reference in PROSE used to report the whole document as unparseable, and every control character and noncharacter a browser KEEPS was deleted from the text the checks read. Rewriting `rawdata` is not an option (every check reads RAW offsets into the original document) and `convert_charrefs=False` would change how every text run is DELIVERED - one `handle_data` per run becomes one per fragment between references - so `_BrowserTextCharrefs`, the base of every parser in the package, re-binds the ONE `unescape` global of the host's OWN `goahead` to `_unescape_text()`: the tokenizer stays byte-for-byte the host's (same run boundaries, same positions, same dispatch, so `code_block_spans()` and `content_marker_scan()` still read exact raw offsets) and only the decode of a run changes. Its NUMERIC branch is the same `_numeric_charref()` end state, so `&#<5000 nines>;` in prose is U+FFFD and the rest of the document stays live; its NAMED branch is `html.unescape`'s longest match, which IS the text rule (`&notit;` is `\u00ac` + `it;`, deliberately NOT the attribute rule above). Should a future host stop resolving `unescape` as a global, the binding is skipped and TEXT falls back to the host's decode - never silently, because the covering tests pin it. The SAME binding reaches every parser OUTSIDE the package that reads a whole document - the theme-contrast scan (CMH-THEME-02), and the authoring and deck tools (`section_hash.py`, `doc_stats.py`, `generate_toc.py`, `new_document.py`, `retrofit.py`, `upgrade.py`, `_favicon.py`, `deck/deck_validate.py`) - through the `tools/_browser_attrs.py` shim, which degrades to the host's own `goahead` on a partial install. Without that, a document the validator now READS still could not be stamped, hashed, counted, indexed, retrofitted or upgraded, which is the same defect one step later. The theme-contrast scan therefore refuses NEITHER shape any more - its start tags share the start-tag base and its text shares this decode - so the "could not be read for contrast" report that stood in for that refusal is retired with it. A NAMED reference resolves only when it is an exact match not followed by `=`, so `id="&notit;"` keeps the literal `&notit;`. Python 3.12 unescapes the whole value with `html.unescape()` and yields `\u00acit;`, which would give one document a different `id`, `class`, `href`, `src`, `content` or `data-*` value per interpreter - and with it a different duplicate-id, link, meta-handshake or companion-resource verdict. The rule and the start-tag attribute tokenizer it runs over are VENDORED beside the base (`_ATTR_CHARREF_RE`, `_unescape_attr_value()`, `_browser_attrs()`, copied from CPython 3.13's `Lib/html/parser.py`, with the numeric branch replaced by `_numeric_charref()`, which follows the HTML tokenizer spec rather than CPython), applied to the RAW start tag text so the host's own decoding is never read, and funnelled through the one `_attrs_dict()` both parsers build every attribute view from. EVERY attribute view in the `checks` package shares that one helper - the `_TagAttrParser` tag lookup, the checklist (`data-cmh-item`/`data-cmh-parent`), notes (`data-cmh-note`) and density passes - because a second, host-trusting view is not merely inconsistent: two spellings of ONE browser value (`&notit;` and `&amp;notit;`) decoded to two different strings on 3.12 and a real duplicate `data-cmh-item` id passed validation. So does every attribute view OUTSIDE that package: the deck validator's active-content / egress and authored-content scans, the contrast scanner's inline-style and element views, and the `doc_stats`, `fix_skip`, `generate_toc` and `wrap_sections` authoring tools all read the rule through the `tools/_browser_attrs.py` shim (which resolves `checks.parsing.browser_attrs` / `browser_attrs_dict`, REFUSES a module whose origin is not the shipped one - `import checks.parsing` resolves through `sys.modules` FIRST, so a host process that already imported some other top-level `checks` would otherwise hand these tools a foreign decoder with no signal - and degrades to the host's own list with the standard missing-tool warning only on a broken install, so such an install leaves a tool degraded rather than dead), so one document is not read one way by the validator and another way by the tool beside it. Two shapes made that concrete: a named reference that is only a PREFIX of the value (`class="mermaid &nbspcm-skip"`) resolved on 3.12 and invented a `cm-skip` token the author never wrote, and a duplicated attribute kept the LAST occurrence where HTML5 keeps the FIRST - which let a decoy `http-equiv` hide a `<meta refresh>` from the deck scan, a decoy `style=` hide the live declaration from the contrast scanner, and a decoy `id="commentRoot"` scope the section wrapping to the wrong element. Those tools share the element BOUNDARIES too, not only the attribute-value DECODE and the attribute SPLIT: each locator and scanner derives from the shared `_BrowserBoundaries` through a second shim, `tools/_browser_boundaries.py`, which resolves the base through the SAME shipped-origin check the attribute shim already writes down (it reads that shim's resolved module rather than re-resolving `checks.parsing`, so the two can never disagree about which module they are reading) and degrades to a host-semantics base with the same API, warned about once, on a broken install. Each keeps its OWN element stack parallel to the shared namespace stack (pushed through `_push_ns()`, truncated through `_truncate_stacks()`, raw text entered through `_enter_raw_text()` from `handle_starttag()` where the namespace is known), exactly as `_TagAttrParser` does, and is fed the document WHOLE. That closes the last place one document could still be two: a `<main id="commentRoot">`, a `<pre class="mermaid">`, an `<h2>` or a `style=` an author only QUOTED inside a raw-text body was a real element to the tool beside the validator - which matters more in a tool than in a check, because `wrap_sections` and `fix_skip` INSERT bytes at the offsets they report, so an element a browser never builds moved the edit into someone's prose. It also closes a fail-OPEN direction in the deck's egress scan: `html.parser` consumes a whole `<![CDATA[ ... ]]>` in every context, so a remote `<img>` a browser really does fetch after the bogus comment `<![CDATA[>` was invisible to it. The deck's egress scan keeps the SAME `<noscript>` departure `_TagAttrParser` makes and for the same reason - a `<noscript>` body is raw TEXT with scripting ENABLED but live markup with scripting OFF, so it is re-scanned in a scripting-disabled pass whose findings are kept; the authoring tools and the contrast scan do NOT, because they describe what the scripting-enabled reader of a commentable-html document sees. It takes that superset a different way, though, and the difference is the point: the deck scan reads the WHOLE body TWICE - once scripting-ENABLED and once scripting-DISABLED (where `<noscript>` is transparent at every depth) - and unions the findings, rather than re-parsing the body the enabled tokenizer carved out. A carved-out re-parse has a SEAM: the enabled tokenizer ends that body at the first `</noscript`, which a disabled one may never reach - it can sit inside a quoted attribute VALUE, or inside a `<!--` comment that swallows it and lets a following `<noembed>` swallow the resource too - so markup straddling the seam reached the re-parse TRUNCATED, the browser EOF rule correctly discarded it, and a remote `<img>` a scripting-disabled browser really does fetch belonged to NEITHER reading. Two full readings have no seam: a reader is on one side or the other, and the union is exactly what either loads. Because the union is a superset the same resource can be found twice, so the findings are de-duplicated. The scan likewise REPORTS rather than passes when the shared boundaries are unavailable at all (a broken or partial install), since the host's own would hide exactly the markup this closes - an egress question must never read "could not look" as "nothing to find". The AUTHORING tools degrade the other way on such an install, and deliberately: they keep running on the host's boundaries, because a degraded edit beats a tool that cannot start. State a tool keys on a stack INDEX - the content region `wrap_sections` rewrites, the heading and `nav.cm-toc` span `generate_toc` replaces, the title container and doc-stats strip `doc_stats` replaces - is finalized from `_truncate_stacks()`, so it ends wherever a browser ends the element: its own end tag, an ANCESTOR's end tag, HTML5's implicit `</p>` / `</li>` close, a foreign-content breakout, or end of input. Only the element's OWN end tag is PART of the span - an ancestor's `</section>` closes it at the START of that tag - and where the span does include an end tag its extent comes from the shared quote-aware scan (`_end_tag_close`, reached through the shim), so `</nav a=">">` ends at the second `>` rather than leaving a stray `">` in a rewritten document. Without that an implicitly closed `<p id="commentRoot">` left a stale root index and the wrapping rewrote the sibling content a browser puts OUTSIDE the root. For the same reason a self-closed VOID tag goes through the start-tag path rather than being short-circuited: `<hr/>` really does close an open `<p>`, and skipping it left a `cm-skip` subtree open over content a reader can comment on. The contrast scan gains the shared start-tag machinery with the boundaries, so an oversized numeric reference in an ATTRIBUTE VALUE no longer makes it refuse the document; the one shape it still cannot read is one in the document's TEXT (decoded by the host's own `goahead()`), which is still REPORTED rather than degraded to no findings. The `_TagAttrParser` lookup shares the element BOUNDARIES too, not only the decode: it derives from `_BrowserBoundaries` (raw-text and comment boundaries, foreign content, the implicit `</p>`/`</li>` close and the end-of-input rules included) and is fed the document whole, because a bare `HTMLParser` consumes a `<![CDATA[ ... ]]>` marked section in EVERY context while a browser treats one in HTML content as a bogus comment ending at the first `>` - so `<![CDATA[><script src="//evil.example/x.js"></script>]]>` left a LIVE external script that `_DocParser` reported and the lookup did not, and the self-contained / offline resource checks read the lookup. Every tag is indexed in ONE pass cached for the last few documents (`_tag_attr_index`), so sharing the (slower, explicit) boundaries does not cost one full document parse per tag asked about, and `validate()` releases that cache in a `finally` when the document's checks are done. The lookup makes ONE deliberate departure from the shared view, and only for the EGRESS question: a `<noscript>` body is raw TEXT while scripting is ENABLED, but with scripting OFF a browser parses it as markup and really does load what it names, so the body is re-parsed and its elements are collected in a SEPARATE index that a caller reads through a SEPARATE function (`_find_tag_attrs_egress()`, versus `_find_tag_attrs()` for the browser's own scripting-enabled view - two functions rather than a flag, so a missed argument cannot silently become a hole in one direction or a fail-open policy check in the other). The re-parse is a scripting-DISABLED pass, in which `<noscript>` is TRANSPARENT (a browser with scripting off parses every nested one as ordinary markup), so the fallback is exactly one level deep and nesting cannot bury a resource below a recursion cap. The self-contained / offline resource loops read the egress view and fail CLOSED on a network `<img>`, `<meta refresh>` or `<link>` hidden there; the CSS egress scans read it too - the fallback pass also collects the `<style>` BODIES it parses, and the offline `@import` / style-block `url(...)` / inline-`style=` `url(...)` scans read those alongside the document and template views (`_find_noscript_styles()` / `_find_noscript_inline_styles()`), because a fallback stylesheet fetches for exactly the reader who cannot run the layer. The two views can also disagree about the REST OF THE DOCUMENT after a fallback body: its end is decided by the scripting-ENABLED tokenizer at the first `</noscript`, but a scripting-DISABLED browser arrives there in whatever state ITS parse of the fallback markup left it in. A seam is resolvable only when that state is the DATA state; otherwise the two tokenizers part company and a reference after the seam is live for that reader while being invisible to BOTH views - `<noscript><style>/* </noscript> */ body{background:url(...)}` (the sheet runs on and fetches), `<noscript><title></noscript><!-- </title><img src="..."> -->` (the reader leaves the raw text at a closer written AFTER the seam and is back in markup, where the enabled view has only a comment) and `<noscript><!-- </noscript><textarea> --><img src="..."></textarea>` (the mirror image, the reader inside a comment across the seam) are all live and were all certified offline-clean. So the guard is the tokenizer STATE, not the identity of the element holding it: an inner parse that ends in raw text OR in an unterminated comment / declaration is REPORTED like the truncated-tag seam beside it. A `<noscript>` that runs to end of document has no disagreement to report (it is the end for both views) and stays clean; the accepted cost is that a fallback stylesheet carrying a literal `</noscript` cannot be certified either, which is the fail-closed reading of an ambiguity a browser cannot resolve at that point either. A a PRESENCE question reads the browser view, because a scripting-enabled browser never creates those elements - a CSP `<meta>` or a `figure.cmh-kql` Run link buried in a `<noscript>` would otherwise satisfy a requirement no reader of the layer can see. A parse that blows up - the document's or a fallback body's - is reported (`_tag_attrs_failed`) rather than left as a silently PARTIAL index, and the self-contained check errors on it, because "no more resources found" and "could not look" must not read the same - the same fail-closed contract `code_block_spans` already has. The re-tokenization is deliberately NOT skipped for a raw tag that carries no `&`: the pre-3.13 host splits attributes with `\s` and `=+` where a browser uses HTML whitespace and a single `=`, so `<div id==x>` and `<div id=a\u00a0b>` split differently there with no character reference in sight; only the DECODE is short-circuited, per value. The raw start tag is accepted only when its own tag name matches the tag being handled (under either fold, and undoing whatever a host did with a NUL - pre-3.13 truncates the name there, 3.13 keeps the NUL, both are still THIS tag), since `html.parser` clears it in `parse_starttag()` alone and a caller outside a start-tag handler would otherwise read the PREVIOUS element's attributes; that mismatch, and a missing raw tag, are the only paths that fall back to the host's list, and neither is reachable from a start-tag handler. The start tag's own EXTENT is vendored as well, so the attribute VALUES are identical on every interpreter outright rather than only for the same raw start tag: `_scan_start_tag()` replaces the host's `check_for_whole_start_tag()` - and the `parse_starttag()` built on it - with the HTML5 tag-open, tag-name, before/after-attribute-name, attribute-name, before-attribute-value, quoted and unquoted attribute-value, after-attribute-value and self-closing-start-tag states, scanned character by character (a regex for "a quote opens a value only after `=`" needs nested alternation, which backtracks exponentially on the untrusted input this parser reads). It has to replace the whole method, not just the extent, because the host also derives the tag NAME with its own `tagfind_tolerant` and falls back to emitting the tag's SOURCE AS DATA whenever its attribute regex stops short of the end it found. The two shapes that made one document two documents: a NUL in a tag name (pre-3.13 stops the name there, so `<script\u0000>` opens a raw-text region that swallows the rest of the file, while 3.13 keeps the NUL - a browser writes U+FFFD in a tag name, an attribute name and an attribute value alike, which is what `_fold_nul()` applies), and an UNTERMINATED quoted attribute value (a browser runs the value to its matching quote, meets EOF, and applies the eof-in-tag error that DISCARDS the whole tag and every character after the opening quote; both hosts instead fail to match a value, re-read what follows as further attribute NAMES, and close the tag at the next `>`, resurrecting elements a browser never builds). EVERY parser in the `checks` package that reads attributes derives from the one `_BrowserStartTagExtent` base that applies it, so no check can disagree with another, or with a different interpreter, about where a start tag ends or what it is called; a start tag truncated at end of input is DISCARDED there, while mid-stream it still asks for more data so an incremental caller cannot lose a tag split across two `feed()` chunks, and `reset()` clears that end-of-input flag so a REUSED parser does not read its next document as already finished. The vendored `check_for_whole_start_tag()` also serves the oversized-reference recovery path below, so the one place a start tag still reaches the host's own machinery draws the same boundary. What a start tag OPENS is a SEPARATE boundary and is deliberately untouched by the vendoring: the shared base enters raw text exactly as the host's own `parse_starttag()` would, because getting that right without a namespace stack is not possible (an `<svg><title>` is an HTML integration point whose children a browser really does build, so calling it RCDATA would hide them), so the tolerant passes keep applying the browser set from their own `handle_starttag()` - where the namespace IS known - and every other parser keeps the set it already had. That leaves one RECORDED RESIDUAL: the namespace-blind passes (checklist, notes, density) derive from the start-tag base rather than from `_BrowserBoundaries`, so they carry no namespace stack and read an SVG `<script>`/`<style>` body as the HOST does - text - where a browser reads markup. It is recorded rather than fixed because it is CONTAINED: those three key only on the author's own `data-cmh-*` attributes and on prose density, and NO resource, egress or self-contained gate reads them (every gate reads `_find_tag_attrs` and `_DocParser`, both namespace-aware), so the failure mode is an authoring finding this document does not report, never a network load it certifies away. The scanner does fold the tag name ASCII-only (clause 7 again), since it is now the code that names the element: `str.lower()` would read `<scrip\u212a>` as a `<script>` and open a raw-text region a browser never opens. The foreign-content bookkeeping the CDATA rule needs (namespaces, integration points including MathML `mglyph`/`malignmark` (which stay MathML only under a MathML TEXT integration point - HTML5 writes no such carve-out for an HTML integration point, so keying it on the generic flag put an `mglyph` under `annotation-xml[encoding=text/html]` in MathML and consumed a whole `<![CDATA[` section a browser reads as a bogus comment, hiding the live markup behind it; and the `annotation-xml` encoding is matched EXACTLY, ASCII-case-insensitively and untrimmed, because a browser does not trim it either - one leading space is the difference between an inert MathML subtree and live HTML, and stripping it read an inert `<script>`/`<link>`/`#cmhAssetBanner` as the layer's own live markup) and an `<svg>` inside a non-HTML `annotation-xml`, breakout tags, and a self-closing foreign element - which is RECORDED as an element but never left open, its depth-keyed state closed at once so an empty #commentRoot cannot swallow its later siblings, as for a void element) and the HTML5 implicit `</p>` / `</li>` close (whose scope boundary is namespace-aware, so an HTML element merely NAMED `<desc>` stops nothing while an SVG `<foreignObject>` does) now live in that one base too, on a namespace stack kept parallel to each parser's own element stack, so no truncation path can leave the two views out of step. The marker view (`content_marker_scan`, CMH-VAL-20) blanks EVERY raw-text body rather than only `<script>`/`<style>`, so a region marker quoted in a `<textarea>` or the print `<noscript>` is not a boundary there either. `_DocParser` is fed the document WHOLE (`parse_document()`), which is what lets the unterminated-comment rule resolve the browser's way; `close()` marks the input final too, so a caller that feeds incrementally gets the same resolution. (10) END OF INPUT is resolved here as well, for the whole family the host changed in CPython 3.12.11 / 3.13.5 (gh-135462): a raw-text element still OPEN at EOF has its remaining body flushed (an unclosed `<style>` runs to end of document in a browser, so its rule is live - an older host stalls in cdata mode and leaves that tail in its own buffer, which emptied the body and hid a live unscoped rule from CMH-VAL-20); a HEADING still open at EOF is finalized the same way, through the one helper the end tag uses (a browser renders `<h2 id="sec">Title` at the end of a truncated document, so dropping it lost that document's LAST heading - its text, id and top_level/in_lede flags - from every heading-derived check: the id scan, the TOC/anchor scan and the heading path a comment anchors to); a heading an ANCESTOR's end tag closes ends there too, and so does one a NEW heading start tag pops off the current node (`<h2 id="a">A<h2 id="b">B` is two headings, HTML5's h1-h6 start-tag rule - and that pop is STRUCTURAL, keyed on the current node BEING an h1-h6 rather than on this parser having RECORDED it, so a heading inside a `cm-skip` subtree is popped too: keyed on the record instead, an unterminated `cm-skip` heading stayed open and the VISIBLE heading a browser renders after it inherited the skip and disappeared from every heading-derived view), for the same reason - a browser ends it, so the prose that follows belongs to no heading and the next heading is a heading of its own, where a heading left capturing swallowed the rest of the document, and the following heading's id with it, into one title (and the two AUTHORING parsers that keep their own element stack read those same three heading boundaries: `generate_toc.py`'s `_TocParser`, which builds the table of contents and injects the heading ids - where the h1-h6 pop is not level-matched, an `<h4>` ends an open `<h2>` even though only h2/h3 are listed - and `section_hash.py`'s `_SectionParser`, which slices the review-tracking sections and the document content hash, and which additionally ends the CONTENT ROOT when an ANCESTOR of it closes rather than only on the root's own end tag, so a document truncated before `</main>` no longer folds the text and the headings a browser puts outside the root into the last section's hash - `</body>` and `</html>` are the exception there and do NOT end it, because HTML5 only switches insertion mode on those and appends what follows to the element still open, which in such a document is `#commentRoot` itself; that parser's binding contract is the RUNTIME (`cmhDocContentHash` in a real browser), not the validator, so it follows the browser wherever the two would part, and the after-body case is exactly where they still do - the validator and the table of contents now preserve that same open root too, while a same-named foreign element still closes normally); a TRUNCATED tag is DISCARDED, whether it is a raw-text closer (`</script data-`), a start tag or an end tag, because EOF inside a tag is the HTML5 eof-in-tag error and the characters are not content - an older host hands that source back as DATA, which is enough to plant CSS in a `<style>` body or forge the layer's ready token in a `<script>` body; an unterminated `<?` or `<!` bogus comment (like an unterminated `<!--`) runs to the end of the document as COMMENT data rather than leaking back out as prose; and `</` is resolved as a browser resolves it - TEXT when nothing follows it, and a BOGUS COMMENT when what follows is not a tag name (`</ junk`, `<//`), never a silently dropped tag. That holds for a TERMINATED one too, and is decided HERE rather than delegated: `html.parser`'s `endtagfind` allowed whitespace after `</` before 3.13, so `</ main>` was an END TAG on an older interpreter and a comment on a newer one - one document with two element stacks, and so two cm-skip ancestries, two `#commentRoot` scopes and two raw-text bookkeepings. `</ p>` and `<//>` are therefore bogus comments ending at the first `>` (the markup after that `>` is LIVE) on every host, and `</>` alone is the one invalid shape a browser emits NOTHING for; an unterminated one runs to the end of the document and raises `eof_unterminated` like every other unterminated construct. Only those END-OF-INPUT readings wait for the input to be final - a TERMINATED `</` + junk + `>` split across two `feed()` chunks lands as one comment the moment its `>` arrives. The body flush runs AFTER the host's own `close()` and only while still in cdata mode, so a fixed host cannot double-count it, and the tag rules apply only once the input is final, so an incremental caller never loses a tag split across two `feed()` chunks. Because every boundary is applied rather than inherited, a document validates identically on Python 3.12 (what CI runs) and 3.13+ (what many contributors run), and on every PATCH release of those. That last part is NOT left to the runner: the EOF rules are pinned by tests that reproduce a buffering host IN PROCESS (neutering the host's own `close()`) and that drive each parse hook directly, so each rule is red on ANY interpreter if its production line is removed. Relying on CI happening to schedule a pre-3.12.11 image would be a guarantee that silently expires when that image is refreshed - the split was how the divergence was FOUND (the two runner images resolved different 3.12 patches), not how it is guarded. The three tag handlers themselves are SHARED too, not just the boundary rules they apply: `handle_starttag()`, `handle_startendtag()` and `handle_endtag()` live on `_BrowserBoundaries` (and, identically, on the degraded `tools/_browser_boundaries` fallback) and drive overridable hooks - `_visit_start()`, `_push_element()`, `_visit_void()`, `_after_start()`, `_visit_self_closed()`, `_visit_end()` - so every parser here and every rebased tool outside this package overrides only what it COLLECTS. The sequence (browser tag name, browser attribute dict, child namespace, implicit `</p>` / `</li>` close, the void and foreign self-closing carve-outs, `_push_ns()`, `_enter_raw_text()`, and, for an end tag, the innermost open element then `_truncate_stacks()`) was maintained in eleven independent copies, and one forgotten `_enter_raw_text()` would have parsed a `<script>` body as markup - the base deliberately disables the host's own `_enter_cdata_mode()`, so nothing else would have caught it. A guard fails any subclass that writes a tag handler of its own; the ONE allowed exception is the prose-density pass, which keeps no namespace stack and applies no implicit close (a documented foreign-content gap), so sharing the sequence would change what it counts rather than share it.; and (11) a `class` is read the way HTML reads one, through ONE shared reading rather than Python's own defaults. The TOKENIZING is `html_ws_tokens` (ASCII whitespace ONLY, in order) with `class_tokens` as the membership set over it, and a RAW start tag is read by `raw_attrs_class_tokens`, which runs the SAME vendored start-tag tokenizer the parsed views are built from (clauses 7 and 9) so the raw and parsed answers cannot differ. Every check in this package, the contrast scanner, the deck tools and the authoring tools go through those three (the ones outside this package via `tools/_browser_attrs`, each with the same partial-install fallback the `rel` reading has, pinned to the shared pattern as TEXT). Four readings diverged from the browser (#1139), and NOT in one direction - which is why they were gate-dependent: (a) the argument-less `str.split()` also splits on the vertical tab U+000B, NBSP and U+001C-U+001F, so `class="cmh-kql-run\u000bx"` - ONE opaque class a browser never matches, never styles and never binds - was read as carrying `cmh-kql-run`, which OVER-detects in a WARNING gate (the reverse-tabnabbing warning fired on a link that is not a run link at all) and UNDER-rejects in a PRESENCE gate (a `figure.cmh-kql` whose only run link carried such a class was accepted, though the reader can never run it); (b) `casefold()` is a UNICODE fold that maps U+212A KELVIN SIGN onto `k` and U+017F onto `s`, so `class="cmh-\u212aql"` became a `cmh-kql` figure for the validator alone; (c) a `class=` REGEX over the raw start tag never DECODED the value, so `class="cmh-&#107;ql"` - a real `cmh-kql` to `classList` - failed the hard KQL run-link gate OPEN, and it also matched `data-class=`, matched a `class=` spelled inside another attribute's quoted value, and accepted ANY of several duplicate `class` attributes where HTML5 keeps the FIRST; and (d) a class matched by SUBSTRING (`class="[^"]*cmh-kql[^"]*"` in `refresh_kql_links`, `class\s*=\s*"([^"]*)"` in the deck scaffold) over-matched a `my-cmh-kql-ish` class, never saw a single-quoted or unquoted one at all, and - having no attribute-name boundary - rewrote a `myclass="foo"` into `myclass="foo active"`. The CASE rule is settled per KIND of token rather than uniformly, because HTML does not treat them alike: a token matched as a CSS SELECTOR (`cm-skip`, `cmh-kql`, `cmh-diff`, `cmh-lede`, `mermaid`, `slide`, ...) is matched by EXACT code points, which is how a standards-mode document matches a class selector and how the runtime's own `classList` reads one, so no fold is left for a look-alike to ride in on; a `language-XXX` LABEL is folded ASCII-only, because the highlighter that consumes it and the runtime's own `language-` pattern both fold it that way and an exact match there would have made `class="language-KUSTO"` a Kusto block to the highlighter and not to CMH-KQL-08. The selector half assumes STANDARDS mode, which every commentable-html document is in (the shell the template, `new_document.py` and `retrofit.py` all emit opens with `<!DOCTYPE html>`); a quirks-mode document would fold a class selector ASCII-case-insensitively, and nothing here gates on the doctype. The visible consequence of exactness is that an uppercase or look-alike spelling stops being read as the class: a `<figure class="CMH-KQL">` is not a framed KQL figure, so CMH-KQL-08 reports the block inside it as unframed - what a reader actually sees - instead of demanding a Run link on a frame that is never rendered, and a `<pre class="CMH-DIFF">` is not an authored diff block, so the escaped-diff-text gate no longer applies to it. A raw reading is only as good as the text it is handed, so the scans that feed it slice a start tag QUOTE-AWARELY: a `>` may sit inside a quoted attribute value, and a `[^>]*` slice truncated `<pre title="a>b" class="cmh-diff">` before its class, which skipped a HARD gate on an element a browser really does render as a diff. The `figure.cmh-kql` gate and the authoring Run-link refresh walk START TAGS and filter by class BEFORE slicing a body, because substituting over whole `<figure>...</figure>` spans consumed a plain OUTER figure through the first `</figure>` and never saw a real KQL figure nested inside it. These readers stay deliberately FUZZY or narrower and are NOT class-token matches: `cmhval/contrast.py`'s `_connectorish` (a substring heuristic over a mermaid-generated class label), `deck_validate.py`'s structural `class="..."` self-checks over the deck's own generated markup (issue #1159), `kusto/kql_highlight.py`'s `_RUN_HREF_RE` / `_CODE_INNER_RE` literal rewrite anchors (issue #1160), `authoring/recommend_kind.py`'s `cmh-callout` substring heuristic, and `deck_scaffold.py`'s `CLASS_RE`, which LOCATES the attribute for a rewrite while its VALUE goes through the shared ordered reading. The shared READING has a matching shared WRITING: `_browser_attrs.serialize_start_tag()` is the ONE place a tool that rebuilds a start tag from parsed pairs re-serializes it - the deck scaffold's slide rewrite, the KQL run-link refresh, and the content-root stamps in `authoring/new_document.py` and `authoring/retrofit.py`. Each of those four kept its own copy of the rule and they drifted, so #1191 fixed ONE and left three carrying the identical fusion bug: a VALUELESS attribute written back as a BARE NAME drops the `/` HTML uses to terminate an attribute name, and the NEXT attribute - whose name legally begins with `=` (the unexpected-equals-sign-before-attribute-name state) - FUSES into it and gains a value the input never had (`data-a/=onclick` written as `data-a =onclick`, which re-parses as `data-a="onclick"`). The shared writer emits every attribute in the one canonical ` name="value"` shape and a valueless one as `name=""` - the same attribute to a browser, since an absent value IS the empty string, and one no following `=` can terminate - and escapes each value exactly ONCE from its DECODED form, so a caller reading through `raw_attrs_pairs()` round-trips rather than double-escaping an authored `&amp;`. A guard test pins that each of the four rewrite entry points invokes the shared writer at RUN TIME and RETURNS what it produced (a source grep would pass on a comment or on dead code, and a call-count spy would pass on a caller that invoked the shared writer and then returned a hand-built tag), so the next fix cannot land on one of four again (#1195). The writer also re-emits a source tag's own self-closing ` /` on request: dropping it UN-CLOSED a foreign self-closing element (`<rect .../>` inside an inline `<svg>`), which turns the next sibling into its child and stops it rendering. That is safe only because every attribute now ends in `"`, so a trailing ` /` can no longer terminate an attribute NAME. That escape has a TEXT TWIN: `_browser_attrs.escape_text()` lives beside `escape_attr_value()` and applies the SAME CR rule for a text run, because input-stream preprocessing folds a CR in TEXT exactly as it folds one in an attribute value. It differs in one place only - it does NOT escape `"` or `'`, which are ordinary characters in text a reader would otherwise see spelled `&quot;` - and it repeats the same U+FFFD fold, where NUL matters more rather than less because `<title>` is RCDATA and the tokenizer folds a NUL there, so writing one literally would make the value depend on where it landed. Both take `str` or `None` only, both are TOTAL over the code-point space and deliberately lossy for those two code points, and both carry the SAME precondition. The pair exists because the shared writer alone reaches only the RE-SERIALIZERS: the value GENERATORS - the places that build a value from a CLI argument or a JSON field rather than from one they just read - had their own `html.escape` and so still downgraded an authored CR, which meant ONE `--label` could reach a browser as TWO different values in the same document, `&#13;` in `data-doc-label` and a folded LF in the `<title>` beside it (#1224). They all go through the pair now: `retrofit._root_tag()` (through the shared writer) and `_insert_title_if_missing()`, `new_document.ensure_doc_title()` and its `<title>` substitution, `checklist/checklist_scaffold.py` `_attr()` and its item labels, `notes/notes_scaffold.py` `_attr()` and its seed text, `notes/notes_apply.py`'s cementing write (a text run built from a JSON field, whose `html.escape` also made its own change-detector compare a written `&#13;` against a freshly escaped literal CR and rewrite on EVERY run), `blocks/diff_block.py` (`data-diff-label`, `data-diff-lang`), `blocks/chart_block.py` (the figcaption text and the ARIA label), `blocks/highlight_code.py` (the language class), `authoring/_brand_profile.py` (`data-cmh-brand`), `kusto/kql_highlight.py`'s caption title (`argv[3]`, written as the copy button's visible text beside the `data-cmh-copy` attribute the same caption already escaped correctly), `authoring/doc_stamp.py`'s `set_meta()`, which HAND-ROLLED its own `&`/`"`/`<`/`>` escape - a third private copy of the rule (#1195) - and so stamped a `--session-id` carrying a CR literally, and `deck/deck_common.py`'s `esc()`, which `deck/pptx_to_fragment.py` builds a slide title and paragraph text from a JSON field with - and whose one ATTRIBUTE caller, the image `src`, moves to `escape_attr_value` because the text rule leaves a `"` alone and that would end the value. THE REMAINING PLAIN-ESCAPE WRITE SITES ACROSS THE WHOLE TOOLS TREE, in full, each with its reason - the list is exhaustive so a later panel can CHECK it (one grep for `html.escape` AND for the `html_lib.escape` alias over `tools/`, which is what an earlier version of this list missed) rather than re-derive it. Four are provably CR-free at the write: `deck/_deck_theme.py`'s theme label, which the theme loader REFUSES outright when it contains a CR (`[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9_-]{0,39}`); `blocks/_highlight_core.py`'s `esc()`, which escapes CODE BODY text every caller has already newline-normalized; `blocks/diff_block.py`'s diff BODY, which `_normalize_newlines()` folds to LF on the line above it; and `authoring/doc_stats.py`'s overview `aria-label`, which is synthesized entirely from integer counts and fixed words, so no input character reaches it. The fifth is a REAL residual and is deliberately NOT half-fixed: `authoring/generate_toc.py` writes the TOC `href` with the plain escape (its link TEXT beside it is safe by a different mechanism worth recording - the heading capture collapses whitespace, so a CR never reaches it), and a heading `id` authored as `a&#13;b` therefore round-trips to a link a browser reads as `#a\nb` while the heading still reads `a\rb`. Moving it onto the pair ALONE would make it worse, not better: `generate_toc` reads its document with `newline=""` and splices by OFFSETS into that raw text, and the PARSED attribute view it takes the id from (`attrs_dict`) does not apply input-stream preprocessing the way `raw_attrs_pairs` does - so escaping without the fold would turn a literal CR that a browser had already folded to LF into a `&#13;` the input never meant, which is this bug inverted. What that site owes is the READ-BOUNDARY pairing, `preprocess_input_stream` plus the escape, which is #1195's lane and needs the offset arithmetic reworked with it. (`authoring/upgrade.py`'s `data-doc-source` span rewrite was the same shape and is already DONE - it pairs `preprocess_input_stream` at its read boundary with `escape_attr_value` on the write, which is exactly what a span rewrite over a `newline=""` read owes.) THREE wired sites cannot actually CARRY a CR and are on the shared rule for uniformity rather than for a behavior change, recorded here for the same reason: the language class passes through `_class_language()`'s `[^A-Za-z0-9_+.-]` reduction first, the chart ARIA label is a DERIVED value `derive_aria_label()` collapses whitespace in (the figcaption text beside it is the one that keeps the CR), and a checklist ITEM label is split out of a line-oriented outline - what the text escape buys at those three is only that a `"` is no longer spelled `&quot;` to a reader. A deck slide's title and paragraph text are NOT in that group, though `slides_to_fragment` `.strip()`s them: stripping trims the ENDS, so an INTERIOR CR is carried and the shared text escape is load-bearing there. Two destinations DIFFER from their siblings by a deliberate DISPLAY normalization rather than by escape drift, which is also pinned: `new_document.ensure_doc_title()` `.strip()`s the label before writing the lede `<h1>`, so a leading or trailing CR reaches the heading as nothing while `data-doc-label` and `<title>` keep it; and the note WIDGET's text model is LF-only by design (a `<textarea>` value is normalized to LF by HTML itself, and `assets/js/37-notes.js` normalizes to match), so writing `&#13;` keeps the SOURCE faithful and consistent with `notes_apply` without claiming the running widget holds a CR. The one visible serialization change is that a `"` in a generated `<title>`, lede header, checklist item, note seed, deck slide or KQL caption is now written literally instead of as `&quot;`; a browser reads the same value either way. DECLARED LIMIT, recorded because this change is what makes it reachable for TEXT: the rule above is about the WRITE, and a `&#13;` does NOT survive a RUNTIME DOM export. The export paths re-serialize through `getHTML()`/`outerHTML` (`cmhSerializeElement`), whose fragment serialization escapes `&`, U+00A0, `<` and `>` and never a CR, so an exported copy carries a literal CR that the next load folds to LF. That has been true of ATTRIBUTES since the escape landed, but `escape_text` is the first thing in the tree to put `&#13;` in a TEXT node, so the text arm is NEW here and is not dismissible as pre-existing. Preserving it needs a CR-aware serializer across the export paths, which is separate work tracked as #1225; nothing in this row claims export round-trip fidelity. The RUNTIME counterpart of the CR half of this rule is CMH-EXP-24: the layer's own serializer (`cmhSerializeElement`) rewrites a CR back to `&#13;` on the way out, so a value these writers stamped is not silently downgraded to LF again by the first export that re-serializes the DOM (HTML's fragment serialization never escapes a CR, and the next load's preprocessing folds a literal one). What genuinely remains outside BOTH halves is a CR inside a FOREIGN-content `<style>` or `<script>` body, which that walk leaves verbatim on purpose rather than risk corrupting a real raw-text body. |
